Global Outlook

# “As goes America, so goes the world”

We prefer global equities to fixed income once again, as rate cuts are starting around the globe and joblessness is still low. But investors may elect to sit on the sidelines for now, awaiting clarity that will emerge from the US presidential election.

## Foreword

# "As goes America, so goes the world"

The US will dominate investor focus over the next eight weeks, for both macro and political reasons. The West is still on track for a soft-ish landing; a global trade war looms as the most visible risk, dependent on the outcome of November 5.

FOCUS

Ajay Rajadhyaksha<sup>(i)</sup>
+1 212 412 7669
ajay.rajadhyaksha@barclays.com
BCI, US

##  Listen

The US economy has long been the world’s largest. But other countries have often been more important for financial markets. In 2017, a wave of Chinese pump-priming sparked a ‘Goldilocks’ year of global growth.  A few quarters before that, in H2 15, the dominant theme was China’s capital flight and eforts to stem it. Europe’s sovereign crisis dominated 2012-13; so much so that Greek politicians briefly became household names. Before that was the 2009-12 commodity super-cycle, driven by China’s epic credit growth after the GFC. But since COVID, global macro has been all about the US. Investors have laser-locked on the speed of the US recovery, the aggressiveness of its central bank, and the exceptionalism of its tech firms. This focus has not changed even in late 2024, as hikes give way to cuts. And for good reason.

The US presidential elections are just eight weeks away, with far-reaching consequences for the world. A second Trump presidency would likely see a new global trade war; the former president has promised blanket tarifs of 10% on all US imports and 50-60% tarifs on Chinese goods. Despite 2018’s US-China trade war, the average tarif on all US imports is just 2%, due to heavy substitution efects. But a blanket tarif of 10% would leave the world’s exporters no room to hide; US duties on all imports would rise five-fold, even ignoring China-specific measures. Other countries would retaliate, with uncertain but serious efects on economies and markets. A Trump presidency could also mean far-reaching changes in the US security umbrella, bond markets (if expiring tax cuts are extended), and currencies (given repeated calls for a weaker USD).

A Harris presidency, on the other hand, would likely see most of its legislative proposals stymied. The vice president has not called for new tarifs (which a president can impose without Congress’s approval), while her proposals to raise taxes are likely to run aground in Congress (the electoral map heavily favors a Republican Senate). Gridlock is more likely if Harris wins, with far less dramatic policy changes. But the presidential race is so close, and US politics so partisan, that markets will keep guessing until November 5. The result could lead to starkly diferent winners and losers in global markets.

This is a Special Report that is not an equity or a debt research report under U.S. FINRA Rules 2241-2242.

(i) This author is a debt research analyst in the Fixed Income, Currencies and Commodities Research department and is neither an equity research analyst nor subject to all of the independence and disclosure standards applicable to analysts who produce debt research reports under U.S. FINRA Rule 2242.

And then there is the macro outlook. Once again, the US stands out against China and the euro area. China in particular has had disappointing data after a strong Q1. Household incomes and wages are slowing, and August data show a deeper contraction in durable goods sales and softer services spending. The housing market continues to weaken despite several supportive policies. Falling tax revenues suggest declining profit margins and a weaker labor market, factory activity has contracted for four straight months, and the country is struggling with deflation. The one bright spot has been exports, but a trade war risks those gains. We expect about 4.5% growth in the second half, after the economy contracted nearly 2% (q/q saar) in Q2.

The euro area had a cyclical recovery in H1, but we are skeptical that it will endure. August flash PMIs were boosted by the Paris Olympics, but sub-indices in manufacturing and services suggest below-trend GDP growth. Germany continues to underperform its peers, with the composite PMI declining for three consecutive months. Eurozone manufacturing is under pressure from Chinese exports, especially in the auto sector, and a new trade war could add to Europe’s woes. For Q3 and Q4, we expect the economy to grow just over 1% (q/q saar). India and Japan have been success stories in recent quarters; both equity markets have been standouts YTD. But neither economy is large enough to be a decisive engine for world growth. So the fate of the post-COVID recovery rests largely on US shoulders.

A US recession at this point – with no other growth drivers in sight – would be a serious problem for the world. Fortunately, we think the US expansion is intact. Yes, the jobs market has slowed from 2023’s pace, with the jobless rate rising 80bp from the lows. And yes, household excess savings have largely been used up. But aggregate household incomes are still rising, fueling consumption. US consumers have the tailwind of a massive wealth efect; household wealth is up a staggering \$40-odd trillion since the start of COVID. Fears of a new surge in inflation have faded, allowing the Fed to start cutting, even with the jobless rate low. AI spend by large tech firms has been so large that it now matters for US growth. And financial conditions have eased from early August (higher stocks, lower bonds, weaker USD). We see the US growing at 2.6% across CY 24. We have a relative slowdown penciled in for 2025, but much will depend on the outcome of November 5.

In sum, a ‘soft-ish’ landing is still within sight in Western economies. In particular, there has been considerable progress on inflation in recent months. Global equity markets made a new all-time high recently. In late August, Nvidia reported stellar earnings, yet somehow disappointed sky-high expectations. But more impressive, the S&P500 eked out positive returns even though NVDA fell sharply – a very positive sign. And fears of a bruising USD rally have faded after August’s reversals in the USDJPY and USDCNY crosses. The macro outlook is still supportive of risk assets.

But there are a couple of flies in the ointment. First, expectations of double-digit earnings growth for the S&P500 in 2025 seem too optimistic, especially with nominal GDP coming of with headline inflation. Second, the policy paths post-November 5 are so binary that as elections near, many investors will probably move to the sidelines and wait for clarity. Markets seem to lack conviction going into Q4, and frankly, so do we. Our bias is to be overweight risk assets, short core fixed income, and own the trade-weighted USD. But we would not be surprised if all markets are range-bound in the coming weeks as they wait and watch economic and political developments in the US. For the rest of this year at least, as America goes, so goes the rest of the world.

One thing is for sure: the last quarter of the year is set to be an exciting time in markets and will bring both risks and opportunities. It is our hope that this publication helps you, our readers, navigate financial markets successfully in the months ahead. Happy reading.

Ajay Bayd

Macro Outlook

## All eyes on the US

All eyes are likely to be on the US as the elections approach. The macro outlook is still reasonable: jobless rates are low, inflation has eased, and global rate cuts are starting. If the world avoids a global trade war, a soft-ish landing is still likely.

• The next eight weeks are likely to be dominated by US developments, both macro and political. The US elections are being closely watched; economic policies could be very diferent, depending on the results. We assume a Republican Senate would prevent a Democratic administration from carrying out most campaign proposals, such as lifting the corporate tax rate and taxing unrealised capital gains.

• If former President Trump wins, we expect he will also look to Congress to extend expiring tax cuts. But in areas such as trade and immigration, a Republican administration could make changes without Congressional approval. If the US does impose blanket 10% tarifs on all imports and 60% tarifs on China, the outcome likely would be a lose-lose for the global economy, including significant downside risks for the US.

• If the world avoids a global trade war, we think the West is still on track for a soft landing. Admittedly, the euro area recovery is a bit fragile, while China has continued to disappoint. But we feel that fears of a US recession are overblown; the economy is slowing, but slowly; rate cuts are starting around the globe and jobless rates are still low; and the inflation picture has improved enough that central banks have room to move quickly if needed.

• We see the global economy growing at 3.2% in 2024, around the same as in 2023. The US is likely to maintain last year's pace and grow at 2.6%, while the euro area should be a bit stronger than 2023 (given the weakness in H2 23), at 0.7%. We expect China to slow to 4.8%, while India should remain a growth stalwart. Beyond early November, though, much depends on the approach the US takes going into 2025.

The third quarter of 2024 has had its ups and downs. Fears of a second surge of inflation in the US have faded. Rate cuts have started in the UK and the euro area, and the Fed likely will kick its own cycle of next week. US equity markets had a massive rotation trade in July, with small caps roaring back – both in absolute terms and against their large-cap counterparts. Early August had a violent unwind of the JPY carry trade, followed by an impressive bounce-back in risk assets.

## A political earthquake in the US…

The biggest surprises of the quarter have come from US politics. Late June had an earlier-thannormal debate between President Biden and former President Trump. Biden’s performance raised concerns about his age and ability to complete a new four-year term, including among members of his own party.

In response, prominent members of the Democratic party, as well as large fundraisers (such as the actor George Clooney) publicly called for a new Democratic nominee. Nearly a month after the debate, on July 21, Biden announced that he would not seek a second term – an unprecedented step. Sitting presidents are almost always their party’s nominee for re-election; since WWII, only Harry Truman and Lyndon Johnson have been the exceptions. Even more unusual, he dropped out less than four months before the elections.

A second twist came when the Democratic party coalesced very quickly behind Vice President Harris as the nominee, avoiding a nomination fight. Fundraising has gone up sharply since Harris took over, and the polls have shifted considerably as well. At the time of writing, the US presidential elections look likely to be extremely close – a far cry from how the race looked in the first half of July.

## … makes November 5 an extremely important date

We are normally skeptical of letting geopolitics and politics drive market views; we believe that macro data are usually more important. As an example, oil markets have struggled to rally since the events of October 7, despite continued fighting in the Middle East, tit-for-tat strikes between Israel and Iran, Red Sea shipping lane attacks, etc. Clearly, the geopolitical risk premium has mattered less over the past several quarters than macro developments such as weaker Chinese demand and Iranian oversupply.

Similarly, US presidential politics can make for interesting conversation, but the actual efect of a change in administration on the economy is often smaller than expected. Within the United States, power is difused. Congress has power of the purse, most important legislation needs 60 votes in the Senate to advance, states have more power than in other countries, and an independent central bank matters enormously for financial markets. But a president has more leeway on matters outside the US, including in the areas of national security as well as trade and tarifs.

## Harris's proposals need Congressional approval and face a high Senate hurdle…

As a result, November 5 is very important for the macro outlook. Both campaigns have put out proposals that could – if actually enacted – afect the economy. For example, the Harris campaign has talked about raising the corporate tax rate to 28%, which would immediately hit US equity earnings and likely be received poorly by the stock market. The proposal to tax unrealized gains of high net worth taxpayers could push investors to private assets while penalizing public assets, reduce liquidity and transparency in financial markets, and undermine risk-taking in the economy; it could also be extremely dificult to enforce. As economists, we are skeptical of the idea that price-gouging has been a big driver of food inflation and do not expect price controls to push down grocery bills. Giving down-payment subsidies to prospective homebuyers is likely to push up the average price of a home, rather than significantly increase homeownership rates.

On the Republican side, the former president and his VP nominee Vance have called for weakening the US dollar as a way to boost exports. We fail to see how an administration can make this happen, short of co-opting the central bank and forcing it into overly easy monetary policy. The Trump campaign has talked about ending illegal immigration and deporting millions of illegals currently in the country. But the surge in immigration has boosted economic growth, pushed down inflation, and let the economy grow for longer without overheating. Moreover, the practical challenges of deporting millions of people already in the country are immense.

We realize that these are all campaign promises, which often involve a degree of hyperbole – on both sides of the aisle. But to date, the Harris campaign has put forward proposals that require Congressional approval – and that seems very unlikely, due to the Senate electoral map. We are not political analysts, but to make a call on policy, we need to have a view on the post-November 5 composition of Congress. Our working assumption is that the Republicans will win the Senate.

The Senate is currently 51-49, with 23 Democrats (and just 11 Republicans) up for re-election. Senator Joe Manchin (now an independent) from West Virginia caucuses with the Democrats. He is not running for re-election, which means West Virginia’s Senate seat will be very dificult for Democrats to defend. Montana is another heavily Republican state where the sitting senator is a Democrat (and more than five points behind in the Realclearpolitics average of polls). In general, the sheer number of seats that Democrats have to defend (and the states they are in) argues for a Republican Senate. The House is a much closer call. Regardless of who wins it, a Congress even partially controlled by the Republicans is likely to stymie any far-reaching policy shifts planned by a Democratic administration. As a result, if Harris wins, gridlock seems the most likely outcome.

## … but many Trump proposals do not

However, a Trump win could lead to more significant changes for the world. Sure, the former president would also need Congress for some of his goals – most notably to extend the expiring tax cuts passed in his first term. The Trump campaign has also laid out its views on several areas where a president can bypass Congress. On national security, for example, European and Asian allies are worried that a Trump administration might reduce the US global security umbrella, as a means of getting other NATO countries to spend more on defense. The push to curtail illegal immigration could lead to a supply-side squeeze for the US labor market (though that might take some months to show up in macro data).

Perhaps most significant for the macro outlook is the Trump proposal for heavy tarifs. In the "Tarifs: Counting the Costs" and "Tarifs: Cross-asset implications" chapters of this Global Outlook, we look at the possible efects of a global trade war that starts with the US imposing heavy tarifs on its imports. The US ran a \$800bn trade deficit in 2023, including a large goods deficit and a small services surplus. Its major trading partners are the euro area (17% of goods imports), Mexico (15%), China and Canada (both 14%). Before the 2018 tarifs, China was about 20% of US goods imports. But even after the US imposed an average of 19% tarifs on imports from China, data from the USTR show that the trade-weighted tarif on all US goods imports is only 2%, with nearly half of all imports currently free from any tarifs. Substitution efects proved very valuable and allowed US importers to ofset much of the efect of China tarifs.

This time would likely to be diferent. The former president plans for a blanket 10% tarif on all US imports, in addition to 60% on China. Estimating the economic efect of such massive changes is very dificult. There is the direct efect, probable retaliation from other countries, the knock-on efect on business confidence and investment spending, likely spillovers into equity

and bond markets that then afect households, and the USD likely strengthening and ofsetting tarifs to some extent – the ripple efects go on and on and on.

## In a global trade war, there are no winners

In assessing the potential impact of a global trade war, we assume as a starting point that the US puts a blanket 10% tarifs on all imports and a total of 60% on all Chinese imports, and that the world retaliates in kind. This would be a full-blown global trade war, magnitudes larger than the US vs China in 2018. Our analysis concludes that the US would get hit roughly as much as other large economies such as the euro area and China. At first glance, this seems counterintuitive. After all, the US imports more than it exports – the goods deficit is about a trillion dollars. Why, then, would proportional tarifs have negative efects on the US and non-US economies? Then there is the example of 2018. The US shrugged of the 2018 tarif war fairly easily and grew above trend that year.

Substitution efects blunted a large part of the efect in 2018. The currency played a role; the CNY depreciated over 10% against the USD from Q2 to Q4 of that year (meanwhile, Trump wants the USD to depreciate). The US also passed a large tax cut in 2018. The main reason our analysis shows the US being afected as much as other economies is that the scenario above places the country in tit-for-tat tarif exchanges with all economies simultaneously. Meanwhile, none of the other economies is likely to engage in similar tarif wars with each other. For example, the euro area’s exports to the rest of the world are 20% of its GDP, while US exports are 7% of US GDP. But only one-sixth of the euro area’s goods exports are to the US; this 3% of euro area GDP would get hit with 10% tarifs while all 7% of US GDP would get hit with the same tarifs as well. Meanwhile, confidence channels related to trade would likely to hit the euro area more. Our point forecast is for the US, the euro area and China GDP to be hit by 1.4%, 0.7% and 2% respectively in the year following the implementation of tarifs.

These are point forecasts, and they come with plenty of caveats and assumptions. For example, the US could pass a large tax cut to blunt a new trade war. Other countries – especially smaller economies – might actually not put proportional tarifs on the US in response. Also, our forecasts are about the initial efect of a trade war. Over time, diminished global trade is usually worse for smaller, open economies (such as those in Europe) than large, insular ones such as the US. It is also possible that trade tensions eventually spread further, if for example China seeks new non-US markets for its excess capacity. This is admittedly an exercise with large confidence intervals. One thing we can say with conviction is that tarifs are not a zero-sum game, with winners and losers. In a global trade war, however, everyone loses.

There is a significant chance that if Trump wins, he walks back his tarif proposals or at least softens them, especially if equities react poorly. In his first term, the former president equated rising stock prices with successful economic policy; an equity sell-of might convince him to move more slowly. A Trump win likely would also lead to far less regulatory pressure on industry, with a number of beneficiaries (financial services, M&A activity, etc), if his first term is an indication. On the macro front, the first years of the Trump presidency had a tax cut, the appointment of Chair Powell, and above-trend growth. While the US did impose tarifs on China, the administration in 2016-20 also used the threat of tarifs to reform existing trade agreements (like NAFTA) or strike new deals.

Regardless of the policies that come to pass in 2025, it seems reasonable to say that a Trump win would likely kick of far greater reactions in financial markets, given the likely composition of the Senate and Harris's policies requiring Congressional action. Much investor attention over the next eight weeks will focus on parsing every new movement in the polls, especially with the election outcome so dificult to call.

## The euro area’s recovery is fragile

Of course, the other variable competing for investor attention beyond US politics is macro data. Once again, the US is key – Chinese data remain disappointing, and Europe’s recovery is fragile. Although real GDP in the euro area grew 0.3% q/q in Q1 and 0.2% q/q in Q2, the details paint a less positive picture, especially in Germany and France. Gross fixed capital formation declined in both countries in Q2, Germany’s real consumption is still below the pre-COVID level, and the French household savings rate has kept rising and is now several points above pre-pandemic highs. High-frequency indicators look especially weak in Europe’s largest economy; manufacturing confidence is at its lowest since June 2020 (August IFO) and consumer confidence (the GFK survey) is falling. France has done a bit better, with services activity getting a boost from the Paris Olympics. At the euro-area level, employment expectations and economic sentiment are still below long-term averages; data across the continent suggest weak but slightly positive growth.

One bright spot has been the resilience of the labor market. Euro area unemployment set a new record low of 6.4% in July, driven by a 0.4% decline in Italy. The jobless rate is famously a lagging indicator, and Italy’s decline was helped by a drop in the participation rate. More worrying are developments in the German auto sector. Last week, there were media reports that Volkswagen AG was considering factory closures in Germany, for the first time in its 87-year history. This is unprecedented in a country where automakers famously protect domestic jobs; if other car firms (and related companies such as parts suppliers) follow, it could become a macro issue. We do not mean to be alarmist, but there is a real risk that unemployment in Germany rises not for cyclical reasons, but as some core industries finally realize that their business model – cheap energy from Russia and exports to China and the US – has changed. Even absent a US downturn or a new global trade war, the euro area recovery remains fragile.

## China faces a hard slog…

For much of the past decade-plus, China has been the engine of global growth. But the country’s post-COVID recovery has been lackluster, coming in fits and starts. The economy grew strongly in Q1, but has struggled since, contracting 1.9% (q/q saar) in Q2. Domestic demand remained weak in August after a disappointing July, amid reports that manufacturers were starting to scale back. The consumption contribution to headline GDP growth fell to 2.2% in Q2, from 3.9% in Q1 and 4.3% across 2023. This trend has continued in the third quarter, with retail sales growth well below 3% y/y. Auto sales disappointed in August, despite the government's doubling subsidies for trade-ins, and services consumption also declined (as seen in box ofice revenues, freight data, etc).

Fiscal data are a more reliable gauge of the Chinese economy than GDP figures, and the most recent numbers show a broad-based drop in tax revenues. Listed companies’ H1 results also point to a deterioration in corporate margins, with risks for the labor market. Weaker domestic demand is adding to overcapacity, with price wars and price cuts becoming common across industries. Property demand has still not stabilized; new sales in 30 major cities fell deeper into contraction in August, even as second-hand sales also cooled. Despite the May 17 housing policy package, we see no signs of a bottom. The China Index Academy survey in July showed that fears of a further drop in home prices continue to weigh on Chinese households, along with unstable incomes and the pressure of existing loans.

For the past several quarters, exports have been a rare bright spot for China and have supported the manufacturing complex. But in early August, export growth missed the consensus by a wide margin. We see headwinds to exports in H2, amid rising trade tensions. Already, China’s steel exports are set to reach an eight-year high, and its leading automaker BYD recently said it expects 50% of its sales to come from non-Chinese markets. The rest of the world is pushing back. The EU launched an anti-dumping probe into Chinese steel products in May, and Canada recently announced new tarifs on steel and aluminum, as well as a 100% tarif on EVs. In response, Beijing is considering tarifs of its own (targeting EU pork exports, for example) and is investigating Canadian rapeseed exports. As export growth has run into challenges, so has Chinese manufacturing, with the NBS composite PMI dropping for a fifth straight month in August, driven by a fourth consecutive fall in manufacturing PMI.

## … and the risks of a liquidity trap are rising

This rapid slowing of investment and production, weak consumption, a worsening labor market, contracting private loan demand and falling home prices have fueled concerns of a Japan-style balance sheet recession in China. These concerns are very apparent in China’s financial markets; the Shanghai composite index has underperformed global equities by over 15% this year, and Chinese bond yields have kept falling. Core CPI (excluding energy and food) hit a 40-month low in July; the country is still struggling with deflation. The PBoC seems worried enough about the signals from the bond market that it is taking steps to push up longer bond yields.

The risks of a liquidity trap, a la Japan, are real (as we discuss in "China: Escaping Japanification"). The combination of slowing growth and a painful de-leveraging process can hamper an economy for years; Japan took more than two decades to repair balance sheets and break out of disinflation. China’s policymakers are trying to pivot to new drivers of growth, but with questionable staying power. Moreover, Chinese households have sufered a massive loss of wealth from the housing slump, of about \$18trn. The equates to each three-person household in China having lost \$60k since 2021, almost five times per capita GDP. With demographics and trade tensions also headwinds, we think China will struggle to grow sustainably at 5% or higher.

The one piece of good news is that the government has been agile in managing financial system risks. Policymakers have helped property developers stay current on their onshore liabilities (even as they defaulted on ofshore debt), removing the risk that large property developments are left half-finished. Corporate borrowers and SMEs have been ofered banking system support where needed; corporate defaults remain low. The financial system is flush with liquidity, with no pressure on the liability side. The CNY has not attracted the kind of headlines that the JPY did, both amid the USD bull run and in the dollar weakness that started in August; the PBoC seems to have a good handle on currency moves. The tail risk of a financial system melt-down has been efectively cut of, in our view.

## The US is slowing, but slowly

With the euro area recovery fragile, and China facing a liquidity trap, a US recession at this point would be very problematic for the world economy. We understand why such concerns have picked up. Excess household savings have dissipated in the past few years (though we estimate that about \$700bn is still left), and the household savings rate is low. Real rates have risen as inflation has dropped. Structures investment jumped from H2 22 to the end of 2023, helping the construction sector shrug of higher rates. It has flattened in recent quarters, albeit at a high level. Companies are warning that lower-end consumers are increasingly feeling pinched.

Perhaps most important, the labor market has shown signs of slowing. Quits and jobs vacancies in the JOLTS survey, the quarterly Employment Cost Index, and the 3mma of job growth all suggest a labor market that is not as strong as in 2023. Last week’s JOLTS was a case in point, with job openings falling far below expectations. Small businesses are quite gloomy; the NFIB survey of small firms’ hiring intentions has been declining for a while. Most worrying, the US jobless rate has gone up 80bp from its lows.

Despite this narrative, we think the chances of an outright US recession remain low. Consumer balance sheets are extraordinarily robust; both financial and real estate wealth has gone up enormously since COVID. US household wealth is up well over \$40trn since Q4 19. Naysayers will argue that this is disproportionately held by richer households, which reduces its efect. Such a distribution has always existed – and so have wealth efects. When consumers feel richer, they spend a little more. As a rule of thumb, US households spend a small fraction (2-3%) of wealth created over the prior 18 months during the next year. If, say, \$15-20trn of new wealth has been created in the past six quarters, that is a spending boost of \$400-500bn over the next year. Even as the efect of excess savings fades, wealth efects should be an efective ofset. Finally, we also believe that the \$700bn of excess savings left is enough to cushion consumption for a few more quarters.

## The ‘virtuous cycle’ for the US consumer is still intact

More important, the virtuous cycle of employment, income and spending still seems intact. Last quarter’s GDP was revised higher, led entirely by an upward revision to real PCE (personal consumption expenditure), which grew 2.9% (q/q saar) in Q2. Furthermore, real consumer spending grew 0.4% m/m in July, stronger than the pace in June. We think there is a strong carryover efect on the spending front into Q3 (of around 1.4%). Add in July’s strong increase and it adds up to a 2.9% (q/q saar) increase in real PCE in Q3, even assuming no change in August and September. Also notable is that spending outpaced income gains in July, even though personal incomes grew at a decent 0.3% m/m. The savings rate dropped to 2.9%, suggesting consumers are not worried about unfavorable labor outcomes and are in no mood to engage in precautionary savings.

The increase in the jobless rate is notable, of course, and not something to be dismissed lightly. But unemployment has risen not because separations have increased, but simply because labor supply has been so strong. As we discussed in detail in The Sahm Rule: A user's manual, we do not believe that the increase in joblessness over the past year is an indication of recession. Claims data bear this out; continuing claims are nowhere close to the levels that should imply a much bigger rise in the jobless rate. And we are skeptical of the idea that the lags from monetary policy are so long that they still have not had time to hit the US economy. The most rate-sensitive part of the economy is the US housing sector, and mortgage rates are now below the levels they were at in September 2022.

Last Friday’s payroll report admittedly leaves the Fed room for thought, but not alarm. The 3mma of job gains is running at 116k after downward revisions to the prior two months. This is considerably below the 6m average of 164k. On the other hand, the jobless rate fell to 4.2%, average hourly earnings went up, and non-farm payrolls bounced from the July (revised) low print. The payroll proxy (hours worked times average hourly earnings) is up a healthy 4.6% over the past three months, even though it was flat in July. This is slower than the 6.1% average increase in the payroll proxy across 2023, but healthy enough to support continued spending. The US fiscal deficit continues to run at 6-7% of GDP in CY 24, also supporting the economy. AIrelated spend by US tech firms has been so large (several hundred billion dollars) that it should now be a macro line item for the economy. Finally, financial conditions have eased considerably since early August; the USD is weaker, rates are lower, and equities are higher. The US is slowing, yes – but slowly.

## Steady growth, slowing inflation and…

Add it all up, and we see the global economy growing 3.2% in 2024, right on top of our estimate for 2023 (3.2%). The US should grow at about the same pace as last year (2.6%), the euro area should actually be a bit stronger at 0.7% (given the stagnation in H2 23), and China a bit softer (4.8%) than even 2023’s disappointing growth. India has been a stalwart in recent years; that looks set to continue, with credit demand solid, auto sales and freight strong, and the external balance well behaved. Admittedly, we think the risks to global growth are tilted to the

downside, especially in China. A global trade war, if it starts post-November 5, would be a risk to the global expansion.

While questions have risen about the growth outlook, the inflation news has been better, with risks receding across the West. Euro area flash HICP eased 40bp to 2.2% y/y in August, in part due to base efects. Core inflation rounded down to 2.8% y/y; services inflation picked up a little, but was boosted by volatile components (airfares and package holidays). The inflation story in Europe is fairly benign at this point. Services prices have been somewhat sticky (hovering at about 4%), NEIG goods disinflation will soon stop helping, and the bulk of the inflation decline appears over. However, wage pressures are easing, inflation is pretty close to target, and the policy rate is quite restrictive. Core inflation should stay near current levels for the rest of this year, but that should not stop the ECB from continuing to ease.

The same goes for the Fed. After the outsized price gains of Q1, the US has seen an overall moderation in inflation. Core PCE rose 0.16% m/m in July, and we estimate that it will rise 0.18% m/m in August, given this week’s CPI report. The 3m annualized rate of core PCE inflation has slowed sharply, to 1.7% in the three months ending in July, and we do not see a material change once August core PCE inflation is released. Core services ex-housing (what the Fed calls ‘supercore’) is running at 0.2% m/m for the past several months, in line with the pre-pandemic average. Our Q4/Q4 core PCE forecast for 2024 is 2.7%, largely because very benign prints in the second half of last year make the y/y comparison challenging. Our Q4/Q4 call for year-end 2025 is 2.3%, though that forecast assumes no jump in goods prices due to trade disruptions. US core inflation is not going to get to 2% any time soon, in our view, but it is close enough that we think the Fed can start easing.

Meanwhile, Asia’s two largest economies face drastically diferent inflation profiles. In Japan, nationwide CPI rose 2.8% y/y in July, the same pace as in June, but core CPI (excluding perishables) picked up slightly, to 2.7%. The so-called ‘BoJ core’ – excluding perishables and energy – slowed to 1.9%, as did services inflation excluding imputed rent (which is a focus for the central bank). Inflation in Japan should remain bumpy for a while. Services inflation should rise as higher labor costs show up in prices, and goods should increasingly reflect the JPY's firsthalf weakness. Eventually, we expect both trends to flatten; core CPI inflation should be 2.6% for FY24, before slowing to 2% in FY25.

At this point in the global inflation cycle, the biggest challenge might be that China’s inflation is too low. At first glance, the July numbers were welcome news. China’s headline CPI rose to 0.6% y/y in August, near consensus. But core CPI (ex-energy and food) hit a 42-month low of 0.3% y/y in July, and services CPI faltered. Even headline CPI was just 0% if we strip out volatile pork prices. We are also worried that housing rental CPI is underplaying the trends in private data. The widespread moderation in inflation in sectors such as healthcare, autos, housing and travel suggests a real risk that deflation eventually becomes entrenched.

## … an unusual easing cycle

Central banks usually take the escalator up and the elevator down. Rate hikes are measured and happen slowly, while rate cut cycles are fast and furious. This cycle is anything but usual. The speed of rate hikes surprised markets this cycle, with the Fed hiking 75bp each for three straight meetings and the ECB carrying out the only 75bp hike in its history. Similarly, the rate cut cycle has been the exact opposite of normal. Markets first started pricing in quick rate cuts in Q2 23, only to be rebufed. Then, at the start of 2024, fed funds futures expected 6-7 cuts from the Fed by the end of this year. Yet here we are, with fed funds still at the cycle high.

It is now time, though. Chair Powell made it clear that the Fed does not want the labor market to ease any further, inflation data have cooperated, and the Fed believes current rates are significantly restrictive. We expect it to ease starting next week, with 25bp cuts at each

remaining meeting this year. The Fed has plenty of reason to get to ‘short-run’ neutral fairly quickly – to essentially get the first 100-125bp of cuts out of the way, then wait and see how the economy is responding. On the other hand, an initial 50bp cut would immediately raise market expectations that the next few cuts are also 50bp each; communicating the path forward might get trickier.

We expect 75bp of cuts this year, but would not be terribly surprised if the Fed goes a little quicker than our baseline. Our bigger question mark is about 2025, when Fed decisions do intersect with politics. If US goods prices rise sharply next year, we think the central bank will pay attention, even if these price rises are a one-time increase (not dissimilar to a VAT hike). That is not our baseline, of course, which is for another 75bp of cuts next year; absent a significant recession (which we do not expect), we think market pricing of a sub-3% fed funds rate by year-end 2025 is far too aggressive.

The ECB is highly likely to cut rates again when it meets just after our Global Outlook was published, bringing the deposit rate to 3.5% President Lagarde is expected to repeat the mantra of being data dependent, suggesting that the October meeting is very much ‘live’. With services inflation sticky, the economy still expanding, and some diferences in opinion in the GC, the path of least resistance is to keep to quarterly easing. We expect the next cut to be in December, but then assume a somewhat quicker pace, with 25bp cuts in January, March and June and pencil in a 2.5% deposit rate for the end of next year.

The BoJ, of course, is the one central bank going in the opposite direction to other developed economies. It does not seem to be in too much of a rush, and the market upheaval in early August will likely be a factor. We think the hurdle to hikes in the September and October meetings is large, amid major political events such as the LDP presidential election, a potential general election and the US elections in early November. The end of relentless JPY depreciation also gives the BoJ a bit more breathing room. As a result, we expect the next hike to take place in January, followed by another next July that takes the terminal rate to 0.75%.

In sum, our macro outlook is of a world where the global economy – while not at the robust levels of 2021-22 – is still expanding at a reasonable pace. Moreover, consider the central bank cycle: rate cuts do not usually start with the US jobless rate below 4.5% and global equities near a record high. If this is the trough for the global economy, it is not a bad place to be. As long as the world does not plunge into a global trade war, we believe Western economies are still on track for a soft-ish landing.

Investment Outlook

# Investing in an election cycle

The macro outlook remains supportive of risk, with the US expansion intact and cuts starting. We remain overweight equities over fixed income, despite the risk that many investors may move to the sidelines until after the US elections.

For the last several editions of the Global Outlook, we have, with increasing reluctance, stayed overweight equities over bonds. Our hesitation has not been because of the macro outlook or earnings projections, but simply due to valuations. Overall, though, equities have made more sense than bonds for almost two straight years. And we think this is true yet again, even as we enter a period of rising political uncertainty.

After posting returns of more than 15% in the first half of the year, the S&P 500 has largely moved sideways for the past three months, though it touched a new all-time high in August. Unlike H1 24, the equity market has started to shed its dependence on a few mega-tech stocks in recent months. For example, in August, the S&P 500 delivered positive returns despite Nvidia falling, while small caps staged a furious rally of their own. The market also showed impressive resilience after the JPY carry trade unwind of early August. Admittedly, as we write these words, stocks are coming of their worst week in almost 18 months. But our view is that growth fears about the US are overdone and should abate sooner rather than later.

In other words, the macro outlook is still supportive of risk assets – we expect the US expansion to continue (albeit at a slower pace than in the past six quarters), while the risk of a new surge in inflation is low, giving the Fed plenty of room to cut if needed. The central bank has also changed its balance of risks and seems far more focused on preventing a further labor market slowdown. The bond market is overly worried, we think, just as it was at the start of this year and in Q2 23. We are not talking about the very front end; we would not be surprised if the Fed delivered the 100bp of cuts priced in for the rest of the year, in an efort to get to ‘short-run neutral quickly. But the market seems too focused on the tail risk of a sharp and sustained easing cycle, with nearly 250bp of cuts priced by end-2025. Meanwhile, 2024 will mark the second year of a nearly \$2trn deficit, even though tax revenues have recovered. Sooner or later, the Treasury will have to signal higher coupon auction sizes; it seems only a matter of time before investors demand a greater premium to take down rising duration supply.

As we noted in the FX & EM Macro Strategy Quarterly Outlook: What to expect when you are expecting (Fed cuts), we underestimated the market’s obsession with the Fed cutting cycle in recent months and the extent to which it would drive the USD. A quick look at history shows that the dollar front-loads easing-related weakness; the majority of USD underperformance tends to happen in the lead-up to the first cut, and that is what we believe explains recent price action. From a macro standpoint, we feel that US exceptionalism remains as strong as ever – the euro area’s cyclical recovery is mediocre (by US standards) and seems fragile, while China’s

struggles with debt-deflation dynamics and weak property demand continue. We do not see US monetary policy – relative to the ECB and PBoC – as a compelling argument to short the USD at current levels. Moreover, sharp recessions and equity draw-downs (if they do happen, which we do not expect) often lead to safe-haven demand for the USD. In our view, a USD trend depreciation would require investors to assume an end to years and years of US outperformance relative to other economies. This seems a step too far, and we believe the currency is more likely to trade range-bound (or perhaps slightly weaker) before rebounding in coming months.

Just like with the macro outlook, the biggest risk to our equity view stems from the US elections. President Trump has pushed proposals to impose tarifs, cut taxes and restrict immigration, while Vice President Harris has proposed large tax increases and price-control measures. A trade war looms as the most realistic policy risk, since a president can bypass Congress on tarifs. The potential policy paths after the election are so diferent that some investors are likely to stay on the sidelines as November 5 approaches. We are also conscious that consensus expectations of double-digit earnings growth for the S&P 500 in 2025 are more dificult to achieve as nominal GDP cools of due to headline inflation coming down. Nevertheless, we feel the arguments against bonds are far more compelling at current levels. And if this is the trough of the cycle, with cuts starting even as the US grows around trend, investors are unlikely to worry too much about valuations. Our baseline assumes that a trade war is avoided, either because a new Republican administration backs of campaign rhetoric or because Harris wins. As such, with the time horizon being the rest of 2024, we remain overweight risk assets (including equities) over core fixed income.

But what we really prefer in this climate of rising political risk is specific trade ideas from a variety of asset classes. Below, we outline a list of key trade ideas that are based on a combination of our macro views, relative value across asset classes, and the balance of riskreward regarding the US elections.

## Upside risks to the Fed’s cutting path are under-priced: Buy 1y1y USD skew

We believe that a Republican sweep should cause a repricing of both rates and rate volatility higher, while a more Democrat-friendly outcome will cause both to reprice lower. Investors will need to factor in some probability of a meaningful price shock related to trade in a Republican win. Markets are currently expecting CPI inflation to fall to 2.15% and the Fed to ease cumulatively by almost 250bp. With core PCE inflation still above 2%, the Fed is unlikely to have much tolerance for higher inflation even if it is a one-time, tarif-driven move (particularly if inflation expectations move higher).

• Trump has also proposed much stricter immigration policy and a reduction in the labor supply; both argue for a higher inflation profile. He has noted that he would not reappoint Powell as chair of the Federal Reserve Board after his term expires (in May 2026), raising medium-term uncertainty. Further, debt/GDP would likely continue to rise regardless of which party wins, but more so if expiring tax cuts are extended. History shows that rising perceptions of fiscal risk are closely associated with higher rate volatility. On the other hand, Harris’s policies are likely to be closer to the Biden budget that would have significantly raised taxes, posing downside risks to the economy, but also a lower increase in the debt/GDP ratio.

• Rates markets currently have a pronounced downside skew (higher volatility with lower rates, and vice versa) in Fed-sensitive parts of the vol surface such as 1y1y rates – a feature that is associated with downside tail risks outweighing upside tail risks. This pricing has been almost unchanged since SVB and has remained even as the forward 1y1y rate has already

declined to 2.9%. Further, US recession probability forecasts have actually fallen over the past year, and the Fed appears willing to ease policy despite above-target inflation. Given this negative skew, there is only a 10-15% probability priced into options that the Fed will deliver fewer than 100bp of cuts over the next six months. We think risks are more symmetric, and the Fed could potentially be forced into an uncertain pause in the event of a Republican sweep – we maintain a recommendation to buy high strike 1y1y payers vs selling low strike receivers.

## Front-end breakevens are too high for the cutting cycle currently priced in: Sell TII Jan 25/Jan 26 forward breakeven vs. short fed funds futures

The forward breakevens implied by the Jan 25/Jan 26s are at 2.15%, close to our forecasts and within the range at which they have been for the past few months. On the other hand, the Fed is now priced to deliver 50bp more in cuts than was the case last month. There are potential reasons why the market is priced for such an extensive cutting cycle, but these are not consistent with the fact that breakeven markets are priced for a “base-case” inflation trajectory.

One reason for the market to price nearly 250bp in cuts is that it reflects a substantial probability of a scenario in which labor markets weaken substantially, leading the Fed to cut by over 400bp, as it does in typical economic downturns. However, it is unlikely that forward inflation would remain where it is priced if such a scenario were to materialize. On the other hand, in the “base case” where the economy evolves according to our forecasts, we do not think such a deep cutting cycle would materialize. We maintain a recommendation to short the Jan 25/Jan 26s forward breakeven against selling Fed funds futures. We believe there are a couple of market structure-related points that boost the risk-reward of this trade.

• The TIPS market tends to sufer from acute liquidity problems when downside risk scenarios materialize – this leads to breakevens compressing much more than fundamentals alone if an economic downturn were to materialize. The current levels of breakevens do not reflect this downside-risk bias.

• We believe that as the year progresses, the TIIJan26s will cheapen because of supply-driven issues in the 1y sector, putting downward pressure on the TIIJan25/TIIJan26 forward 1y breakeven.

## European equity outperformance is an outlier: Fade it via vol

European equity indices outperformed global peers in the August sell-of; the following rally has been an outlier vs history. For instance, SX5E had a max draw-down of less than 30bp during the August rebound after the carry trade unwind. In fact, this draw-down has been the 2nd smallest in SX5E's entire history (ie, since 1986), beaten only once before, in 1989.

We recommend fading this one-way rally in EU stocks for several reasons:

• The ‘bad is bad’ trading setup for equities implies asymmetric risk for stocks; negative data surprises are likely to hurt much more than positive surprises help. For European equities, the outlook is tougher, with a double whammy from slowing DM + China growth. Indeed, this has been reflected in European cyclical valuations, with the Cyclicals vs. Defensives pair down 7% YTD, but earnings estimates are still holding up. For instance, for 2025, the IBES consensus for MSCI Europe expects near double-digit EPS growth, led largely by Cyclicals, which looks a high bar to clear, if growth and inflation were to fall.

• September is the month that equities perform the worst, with volatility traditionally peaking in October (after subsiding into June/July). Historically, stocks have also struggled around the first rate cut. regardless of the subsequent recession outcome.

• Europe has its own version of the Magnificent Seven big-tech firms in the US. Firms such as Novo Nordisk, Glaxo, ASML and LVMH have driven an outsized share of European equity returns since 2022 and trade at much higher multiples than the broad index. But these companies also have a large percentage of their top line coming from the US (and China). If the risks of a global trade war rise, it is very easy to see these names de-rating somewhat out of trade concerns.

• European equity vols screen low versus global peers, making owning gamma attractive into October. Specifically, DAX vol screens lowest versus peers as well as its own history, making it our preferred index on which to own downside protection in European equities.

## Policy divergence and rate cuts: Short CHFJPY

We maintain long yen exposure against CHF as a carry-eficient hard-landing hedge with attractive risk-reward.

• Fundamentals across the US and Japan suggest that policy divergence between the Fed and the BOJ will remain the path of least resistance, with the Fed potentially reacting to labor market weakness while the BoJ stays more hawkish. This should keep the yen supported in the near term. Evidence of a larger slowdown/recession dynamics would only further yen upside.

• On the other hand, the CHF is too strong, given the dis-inflationary dynamics in Switzerland and the SNB's more limited policy space. There is already evidence of push-back via FX interventions around 0.93 in EURCHF (along with rate cuts, which should continue to a terminal rate of 0.50% if not below), which limit CHF upside in a tail scenario.

## Policy divergence and rate hikes: Short USDBRL

We recommend short USDBRL, as we expect the BRL to outperform within EM in the coming months.

• Brazil spent much of the year in a self-inflicted confidence crisis, where the monetary noise essentially served as a wake-up call that the fiscal anchor is weak. But the coordinated message across the administration and the BCB suggests the peak pessimism has already been reached.

• The fiscal framework remains vulnerable, but for now, we think authorities have done enough to calm markets on this front, as risks for 2024 have subsided.

• Moreover, the BCB is set to start a hiking cycle in September, building carry back into the FX while the rest of the world is easing rates (most importantly, the Fed).

## China in a liquidity trap: CGB 10s30s steepener

We recommend a 10s30y CGB steepener.

• We prefer this part of the curve as its ofers better value, remaining relatively flat compared with shorter tenors.

• As China moves towards a liquidity trap in which consumers and investors refuse or are unwilling to spend, but rather prefer to hoard cash, rendering monetary policy inefective, term deposits and wealth-management products have had strengthening demand. Cash in WMPs ultimately ends up in China’s bond markets, weighing on yields, especially around the 10y part of the curve.

• Separately, the PBoC has attempted to tighten its grip on the CGB yield curve by selling borrowed bonds into the market, which we think puts a floor under back-end yields.

Fed on hold is no longer a constraint for EM central banks: Receive 2y TIIE We think that the start of Fed cuts will allow for more easing from key EM central banks such as the Bank of Mexico (Banxico).

• Banxico has started to timidly ease, and markets have been conservative in assessing the future path of the cycle as President AMLO's reforms make their way through Congress this month. While the fiscal efect remains unclear, Banxico still has ample space to ease.

• Headline inflation has rebounded on higher agricultural prices, but Banxico remains focused on the downward trend in core inflation, while services inflation is showing initial signs of easing.

• Moreover, the Fed will likely deliver a series of cuts, and real rates are quite restrictive in Mexico. FX pressures are unlikely to derail the cycle, in our view, as the bank estimates a moderate pass-through, and it could opt for intervention if the currency takes another hit from external drivers. Our economists expect consecutive cuts, with an eventual acceleration to 50bp cuts. We think markets are underpricing risks of a faster cycle and recommend receiving 2y TIIE.

## Summary of Asset Allocation Themes

## Equities

## Key Forecasts

• We recently raised our FY24 S&P 500 price target to 5600 from 5300, based on 23x our base case FY24 EPS estimate of \$241 (raised from \$235). Macro moderation is likely to be a headwind for ex-Tech EPS growth through the end of the year; we expect this to be ofset by Big Tech continuing to surprise to the upside as it did in 1H. Our target SOTP P/E multiple assigns a slight premium to current SPX ex-Tech fair value (lower rates) and a 34x multiple for Big Tech. While our valuation assumption for Big Tech is high, growth-adjusted multiples are reasonable and we expect the group to earn into its valuations.

• In Europe, we remain broadly positive on equities and expect a soft-ish landing, in part engineered by the global rate-cutting cycle getting underway into 2025. Recent softer data and the JPY carry-trade unwind have injected some volatility into the market, while seasonality and uncertainty about the US election (and potential international implications, eg, on tarifs) are keeping us from adding risk short term. EU politics are not helpful, either, and we remain MW Europe vs. US. Next year, double-digit consensus EPS growth seems like a stretch unless growth starts to pick up, but we remain comfortable with our forecast for midsingle digits. We continue to recommend using cheap optionality for equity replacement and/or downside hedges. Our price targets remain unchanged at 545 for Euro Stoxx (SXXE) and 8300 for FTSE 100 (UKX), implying single-digit upside into year-end.

## Key Recommendations

• In the US, we maintain our Positive outlook on Big Tech and upgraded our outlook on Utilities to Positive. We downgraded our outlook on Energy to Negative, leaving us Negative on Industrials and the commodity-linked sectors; rationale includes weaker earnings growth in 2H24, PMIs, and exposure to negative operating leverage as inflation eases. Sector/stock correlations are low, favoring themes and styles over sectors, in our view. We reiterate Growth over Value (EPS upside and rate cuts), Large over Small (quality, downside hedge and we think small-cap optimism into rate cuts is misplaced), stocks with high reactivity to services spending, and broadening beneficiaries based on Big Tech-like characteristics.

• In Europe, we continue to hold a balanced cyclical vs. defensive sector allocation, with some duration exposure to rates coming down. We have made some changes to the sector portfolio recently, using the early August sell-of to add back to Tech in Europe, at the expense of Consumer Discretionary. We are OW Financials via Banks and Div Fins, OW Tech, OW Utilities, and OW Real Estate. We are UW Energy, Telecoms and Consumer Discretionary.

## Bonds

## Key Forecasts

• Incoming labor-market data kept alive the outlook for the Fed’s quick return to the short-term neutral rate. However, strength in income and underlying activity suggests that neutral is higher than currently priced in. We expect long-term rates to move somewhat higher in Q4.

• We believe rates investors will view the US election outcome through the lens of the monetary and fiscal policy implications. We believe that inflationary and/or expansionary fiscal policies would likely lead to higher rates, with the shape of the curve dependent on which factor dominates.

• EUR duration has been increasingly driven by global factors, with Bunds consequently remaining in the passenger seat. While news on the domestic front on growth has not been encouraging, our thesis has been that for Bunds to break decisively below 2%, the catalyst has to come from the inflation side or global spillovers have to play a role. Otherwise, we expect Bunds to continue to trade the recent ranges in the near term, although the shift in the Fed's reaction sentiment and the weak risk backdrop are likely to act as ceilings for how much rates can sell of. All in all, we see Bund yields not too far from current levels by end-2024, at c.2.20%. Further ahead, we see room for them to drift modestly lower to c.2.00% in Q3 25, bearing in mind weak domestic growth momentum and global factors.

## Key Recommendations

• We recommend fading the outperformance of US 5y5y rates on a cross-market basis against EUR. The rate diferential is now meaningfully below pre-COVID levels, even though the US economy has outperformed the European one at a significantly higher policy rate gap.

• At the European front end, we hold a tactical short in 2yf1y ESTR, which sits at the bottom of the range over the past six months. Elsewhere, we hold longs in the belly of EUR 5s10s30s 1yf, which benefits from a modestly positive carry profile and could benefit from any further resteepening pressures at the long-end of the swap curve. In EGBs, we continue to hold longs in 10y Spain vs. France, with the spread still positive despite a notably more supportive Spanish fiscal policy and net supply dynamics versus France. In addition, we hold longs in 10y Finland versus Germany and 5s10s Portugal/Germany flatteners.

## Commodities

## Key Forecasts

• The oil sell-of has accelerated recently, growing the disconnect with spot fundamentals. Indeed, speculative positioning hit a new low this week, and while fundamentals could certainly deteriorate sharply, there is little hard evidence that this is the case so far.

• High-frequency inventory data remain supportive, and while Chinese demand remains weak, COVID-19-related seasonal distortions likely are exaggerating it. Manufacturing PMIs have softened somewhat, but remain within the past two-year range.

• Market participants seem to have shifted too far to the left in their assessment of risks to the outlook, but owing to a lack of hard evidence in support of that view, we continue to push back against the recent price action with a heavy dose of humility, as it can be tough to gauge markets around inflection points.

## Key Recommendations

• Oil prices are significantly depressed relative to our \$90/b fair value estimate for Brent, which makes us stick to our short put spreads recommendation.

## Inflation

## Key Forecasts

• Front-end US breakevens are cheap to Barclays forecasts, but, in our view, should trade at a discount because of lower liquidity compared with nominals and not enough structural demand. Shorter-end forward breakevens, such as the one implied by the TIIJan25/TIIJan26 pair at about 2.15%, seem mispriced relative to broader macro market concerns of downside economic risk, while longer forwards continue to ofer cheap inflation insurance.

• After a sell-of in Q3, EA inflation markets are priced below but close to the ECB targetconsistent outcomes on a sub-3y horizon, with a moderate inflation risk premium priced over the medium-term horizon. The HICPx front end is cheap vs. our economists’ and the ECB’s forecasts. Further out, EUR real yields and breakevens are cheap on our models, but not necessarily mispriced, given rising risks of a global recession and cross-market valuations. In the UK, we see value in intermediate RPI forwards and back-end real yields, both outright and on a cross-market basis.

## Key Recommendations

• Short the TIIJan25/TIIJan26 forward breakeven hedged with 12th fed funds futures contracts

• 2yfwd3y versus 10yfwd20y US CPI swap steepener

• EUR IL: Long Mar-25 HICPx fixing, part energy-hedged

• UK IL: Long 5y5y RPI; long UKTi 37/44 forward real yield; long 30y UK real yield vs the US

## Credit

## Key Forecasts

• In Europe, we see spreads tighter by year-end and IG outperforming HY, with spot forecasts of 95bp and 315bp, respectively. This constructive view is predicated on a soft landing in the US, with still-sluggish growth in Europe and rate-cuts spurring demand, even as all-in yields have dropped. Short term, supply trends and concerns about the economic picture could be a headwind to spread tightening.

• In the US, we expect spreads to trade a bit tighter by YE, 85-90bp in IG and 290-315bp in HY. Seasonal technicals remain negative in the very short term. However, we believe that the US economy is on the path to a softish landing, credit fundamentals are in a good spot, investors are not stretched, and yields should increase. This should lead to continuing demand for US credit.

## Key Recommendations

• While our index forecasts in Europe tilt bullish, our RV views tilt cautious. We have a slight preference for up-in-quality in IG and HY, due partly to valuations, partly to concerns about growth. Equally, we are cautious on cyclical risk and auto sectors, for both IG and HY, as we see little compensation for growth downside or tarif risks. We continue to favour financials over industrials, based partly on valuations, but also due to fundamentals and supply trends.

• In US IG, we prefer high quality in the long end. We believe that BBBs and cyclicals are not ofering much compensation for the downside risk. In US Lev Fin, we favor HY bonds over lev loans, as the Fed cutting cycle reduces carry on floating paper. In HY bonds, CCCs continue to screen wide to the index, while BBs and Bs trade about in line with each other.

## Emerging Markets

## Key Recommendations

• EM rates/FX: The Fed has been a binding constraint for local assets most of the year, but with Fed cuts imminent, the narrative is quickly shifting . We think the shift to a more accommodating stance is enough for the recent repricing in EM rates to stick and have followthrough in some countries (eg, Mexico, Czech, Israel and India). We see scope for a decoupling between EM rates and EM FX as this plays out, with rates outperforming FX. That is, as central banks ease and carry erodes, there is room for risk wobbles to take USD-EM higher, especially as EM FX reflects very little recession premia.

• EM credit: We expect a broadly range-bound environment, with potential bouts of volatility, and recommend taking advantage of opportunities in idiosyncratic HY sovereigns, including in ongoing/recently concluded restructurings and frontier credits. The balance of risks for SRILAN (OW) remains positive, in our view, and we think that GHANA's (OW) market-implied exit yields leave further room for upside. We also recommend UKRAIN’s B34 bond and have buy/OW recommendations in KENINT, BAHAMA and SURINM, for example. A tug of war between supply and demand is likely to drive performance in lower-beta HY and IG: we upgraded SOAF to MW (from UW), but recently downgraded PANAMA to UW. Heavy and recurrent supply from MEX and KSA, combined with political/geopolitical considerations, leaves us with UW ratings on both. The likely lack of further supply in 2024 should support our OWs in COSTAR and DOMREP. We view spreads in EEMEA/LatAm Corporates as expensive on the whole, but have an OW rating on India HY Corporates in Asia.

## Foreign Exchange

## Key Forecasts

• Continued focus on the Fed’s easing cycle creates scope for modest – and temporary – further dollar weakness. This is because the dollar typically depreciates ahead of, rather than during, Fed cycles and the move has already been sizable by historical standards.

• Over the medium term, we expect the dollar to outperform, especially versus open-economy currencies. Neither poor macro prospects in Europe and China nor US recession risks bode well for these. As regards the latter in particular, our analysis shows that the greenback tends to appreciate against most currencies (except for JPY and CHF).

## Key Recommendations

• In G10 FX, we recommend short CHFJPY, short EURGBP; long AUD, CAD, GBP vs. short CHF, EUR, SEK basket.

• In EM FX, we recommend short USDBRL, long USDCNH, long PLNHUF, long 2m SGDMYR; long BRL, COP, CLP, HUF, PLN vs. short MYR, IDR, CZK, KRW, PHP basket.

Global Trade

# Tarifs: Counting the costs

Tarif increases by the US on China and the RoW would lead to output losses for all. Their size and distribution depend on US trade exposure, retaliation measures and confidence efects. China would be hardest hit, knocking \~2pp of growth, followed by the US and EA. The downturn would lead the Fed and ECB to ease more.

• US tarif increases to 60% and 10% on goods imports from China and the RoW, respectively, as proposed by former President Trump, would be a significant escalation of trade restrictions. They would reverse decades of trade liberalisation, with consequences far beyond the 2018-19 US-China trade war.

• Assuming symmetric retaliation from all US trading partners, but accounting for diferent confidence efects, we estimate efect on the level of real GDP to be -2.0%, -1.4% and -0.7% for China, the US and the euro area (EA), respectively, in the first 12 months. The US and China are mostly hit by the direct impact through the trade channel, while for the EA, adverse confidence efects play a significant role.

• Inflation would rise in the short run, especially in the US, by around 0.9pp, as the negative supply shock from higher tarifs increases prices. It would then revert down, after the tarif increases have passed through prices and as demand weakens over time. In the EA, the inflationary efects would be much more limited (0.1%), while in China the efect of higher import prices would be more than ofset by much weaker demand (-0.1%).

• US monetary policy would initially likely remain slightly tighter than in our baseline of no tarif increases, as inflation rises. But as activity starts weakening, amid trade policy uncertainty and tighter financial conditions, we would expect the Fed to ease policy rates more aggressively, possibly as much as 100bp. We think the ECB would initially keep rates in line with the no-tarif baseline, but would then lower policy rates by 50bp more than in the baseline as activity weakens.

• For the larger and more closed US economy, the negative short-term efect would likely more easily dissipate in the following years. It could be more persistent for the more exportdependent China, EA and small open EM economies, especially if global trade tension were to escalate beyond the initial US tarif increases: for example, the RoW could impose tarifs as protection against diverted Chinese goods flows due to the very high US tarifs.

## Context: A historical reversal of global trade policy

## A fundamental change in the US approach to trade...

The proposals set forth by presidential candidate Trump's economic team imply a fundamental change in how the US would approach free trade. It is based on the view that US's lowering of
