tarifs relative to trading partners (who themselves have remained more protectionist, especially China) has caused the widening of the US trade deficit and loss of US manufacturing jobs. To reverse this, the proposal is to implement a 60% tarif on all Chinese imports and a minimum 10% on all other imports from the rest of the world (RoW). The revenues from these tarifs could then be used to lower taxes.

The aim of this exercise is not to challenge this view, eg, by pointing to the US's persistently large savings investment imbalances and its attractiveness to foreign capital inflows as alternative explanations. Instead, we try to assess the economic implication of the proposed tarif increases, if they were implemented by a new Trump administration in 2025. It would likely take months for the president to be able to impose the tarifs, as the administration would likely need to go through a process of investigations. We assume that the administration would ultimately succeed in implementing tarifs, despite possible legal challenges, just as in 2018-19.

## ... with a historical increase in the level of tarifs...

Import tarifs have a very long history and have indeed been used by US governments as the main instrument of trade policy. The three main purposes were:<sup>1</sup> 1) to raise revenue for the government (ie, about 90% of the federal government receipts from 1790 to 1860); 2) to restrict imports and protect domestic producers from foreign competition (from 1861 to 1933); and 3) to reach reciprocity agreements that reduce trade barriers (from 1934 to 2016).

Average tarifs on total goods imports have fluctuated considerably over time, ranging from 14% to 60% in the 19th century, and declining from about 30% in 1900 to about 7% in 1970, 3% in 1990, and slightly above 1% from 2000 to 2016 (Figure 1). As a consequence of the first Trump administration's 2017-19 trade war with China, the average US tarif on imported goods nearly doubled and was about 2% in 2023. The current proposal would increase the average tarif on all goods to c.17%, thus bringing tarifs back to levels not seen since 1935.<sup>2</sup>

FIGURE 1. A 60% tarif on US imports from China and 10% on RoW would raise average tarifs to 1935 level
![](images/c7188d0e366ce88ac62a16a9dfdcceb715fbde4fcd854addf3de51cc81e60ea4.jpg)
Note: \*Assuming Trump's new tarif proposal of 60% on all Chinese imports and 10% on all imports from the RoW, we estimate an import-weighted average tarif to be c.17%

## ...beyond the 2018-19 US-China trade war experience

Average tarif increased from 3% to 19% on USD350bn Chinese good imports During President Trump's first term, tarifs were already increased against a number of US trading partners, including the EU, Canada, Mexico and South Korea (Section 232 and Section 201) starting in 2017. Some additional tarifs threats (eg, tarifs on autos) were not always followed through, and some of the implemented tarifs subsequently eased in response to retaliatory moves.

The main tarif increases, however, occurred against China (Section 301). Over the course of 2018-19, the US increased tarifs on about USD350bn of goods imports on four diferent lists. For the roughly USD250bn of goods on lists 1-3, tarifs rose to 25%; and on the USD100bn on list 4 to 7.5%. For the afected goods, this implied an increase in the weighted-average tarif rate to 19% (from just 3% before).<sup>3</sup> As of 2023, about 46% of imports from China had no tarifs, implying that the trade-weighted tarif on all imports from China is about 10%.

## Diverted trade, higher consumer prices, stronger USD and output losses

US trade with China has fallen well below the trend that prevailed prior to the trade war. The overall share of total US goods imports from China was 14% in 2023, down from 21% in 2018. The US now imports fewer goods from China that are subject to the 25% tarif, and has instead started importing more of those goods that are free from any tarif. Goods subject to the 25% are instead imported from other countries, just as trade theory would predict (ie, trade diversion).<sup>4</sup> Notably, exchange rates adjusted to the higher US tarifs and the broad tradeweighted Dollar Index rose by 2.7% from June 2018 to December 2019.

Regarding the impact on US output, studies have estimated negative efects in the range of 0.3-0.7% of US GDP.<sup>5</sup> Others point out that nearly all the cost of the 2018 US tarifs was borne by US consumers and importers<sup>6</sup> and estimate that the trade war reduced US investment growth by 0.3pp by the end of 2019, and another 1.6pp of by the end of 2020.<sup>7</sup> Studies <sup>8</sup> find that even beyond the immediate impact, tarif costs continue to be almost entirely borne by US firms and consumers. In general, the response of import values to the tarifs increases in absolute magnitude over time, consistent with the idea that it takes time for firms to reorganize supply chains. However, there is heterogeneity in the responses of some sectors, such as steel, where tarifs caused foreign exporters to drop their prices substantially, enabling them to export relatively more than in sectors where tarif pass-through was complete.<sup>9</sup>

While the experience from the trade war episode during the first Trump administration may serve as an initial reference point for the direction of the economic consequences, it is important to note that the current proposal from presidential candidate Trump for 2025 difers significantly in the magnitude (ie, 60% tarifs on China imports) and comprehensiveness (ie, 10% across the board on all imports from RoW). This why just scaling up the 2018-19 experience would likely underestimate the likely impact of the new tarif policy proposed for a new Trump term.

## Conceptual considerations for a 21st century trade war

## Who trades how much with whom?

Before assessing the efects of Trump's proposals, some descriptive statistics are in order to establish the orders of magnitude. Figure 2 summarises trade in goods by the US, the euro area, and China. It shows that the US is a relatively closed economy, with 7% of GDP in goods exports and 11% in goods imports, running a goods-trade deficit of 4%. In contrast, the euro area is more open, exporting roughly 20% of GDP and importing nearly as much. China's exports are of a similar magnitude (c.20%), but its imports are smaller, implying a goods trade surplus of c.5% of GDP.

FIGURE 2. The US is a relatively closed economy, compared with the euro area and China

![](images/e162fc14b47d911d5697b2240c1c51ede2678fddfdbe63aa0438166938fa9565.jpg)
2023 data
Source: Census Bureau, Eurostat, CNBS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Furthermore, Figure 3 shows that these three economies are highly interconnected, though trade with other countries as a whole remains larger. For example, the US imports c.14% of its goods from China, c.17% from the euro area, and 70% for the rest of the world, including Mexico (15%) and Canada (14%). Meanwhile, c.16% of the euro area's exports and about 15% of China's exports flow to the US.

FIGURE 3. US, euro area and China are highly interconnected
![](images/f4359cdcc317adfa61d057f722bc75decc1db6851ba894ba36bb089494625fc0.jpg)
2023 data. Some columns do not add exactly to 100 due to rounding.
Source: Census Bureau, Eurostat, CNBS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

That said, since the 2018-19 trade war, trade patterns have been shifting against US-China trade. The decline in the share of US goods imports from China, from 21% in 2018 to about 14% currently, likely speaks to the substitution efects brought on by tarifs on Chinese imports. In 2023, the share of Mexico's goods in US imports exceeded China's for the first time since 2002 (Figure 4).

These trade shares also highlight that even though the US economy may be relatively more closed than the EA and China, it would still be very exposed to the tarifs, because it would use them against all its trading partners, who are assumed to retaliate symmetrically. Hence, all its imports (worth 11% of GDP) would become more expensive due to the increased tarifs it imposes, while all its exports (worth 7% of GDP) would become subject to the retaliatory tarifs (Figure 5).

For the EA and China, the afected shares of GDP would be much smaller. Given that only 16% of the EA's exports are destined for the US, exports worth about 3.2% of GDP would be afected by US tarifs (ie, 16% of the EA's total exports of 20% GDP). The equivalent number for China is even smaller, at 2.9% of GDP (ie, 15% of China's total exports of 19% GDP). On the import side, only 2.1% of the EA's GDP (11% of total 19% GDP imports) would be afected by the retaliatory tarif the EA would impose on US goods, and in China it would be a mere 0.8% of GDP, given China's limited imports from the US (only 6% of total 14% GDP imports). These simple magnitudes are important in helping understand the estimates from the trade models.

FIGURE 4. The share of US goods imports from China has fallen since the onset of the trade war
![](images/87e780533bceeb7d5f3f9c898aa8b40854fb2d801f3a89945690c6c54a9dc537.jpg)
Source: US Census Bureau, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 5. A larger share of GDP would be afected by tarifs in the US compared with the euro area or China
![](images/d092f0bb3ebec924d466a099003d1415d6ae95bfe6498f08e17beca550b6c5e9.jpg)
2023 data
Source: US Census Bureau, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## Tarifs in the textbook: Deadweight losses

In theory, tarifs on imports are akin to cost-push shocks, as they raise the cost of imported final or intermediate goods. Conventional models of import demand and export supply suggest that a tarif on imports causes domestic consumers to lose welfare, as: 1) they pay a higher price on the amount imported after the tarif is imposed; and 2) they sufer a deadweight loss from the reduction in demand for imports resulting from the higher price paid. The latter is a welfare loss to the country, while the former is a transfer to the domestic government, and thus not a welfare loss for the country as a whole because it could be re-distributed.

In principle, the country that imposes tarifs could end up better of, by forcing foreign exporters to lower the price on their exports, so that they can continue export to the domestic economy. This, in efect, transfers some of their rents to the domestic government, and  results in better terms of trade for the domestic economy. However, if foreign exporters do not feel forced to lower their prices (very elastic supply), then the domestic economy necessarily loses, as there is no ofset to the domestic deadweight loss.

## Complications I: Substitution, diversion and intermediate-goods trade

While the direct efect of trade tarifs on prices is quite clear, quantifying the dynamic overall efect on prices or activity is much more complicated to assess.

For one, substitution efects can play an important role. It is true that import tarifs tend to lower domestic production to the extent they make the production process more ineficient by raising costs, or reduce the real income of consumers. However, import tarifs can also imply a positive efect on domestic activity, if they encourage a suficiently large substitution of imports toward domestic production, and the domestic country’s terms of trade improves. The direction and magnitude of the activity response is thus an empirical question.

Furthermore, if the same tarif is not applied evenly across all goods and all trading partners, trade flows will be diverted, possibly changing how a country's economy will ultimately be afected. For example, countries like Mexico or Korea may have been negatively afected by some US import tarifs against them, but the much higher tarifs the US imposed on China made their goods still cheaper relative to China's, allowing them to gain trade with the US. Following the 2018 tarifs, one could observe some exports from China being rerouted through other

countries such as Vietnam or Mexico, or Chinese firms establishing production in third countries to export into the US from there.

This also points to the complexity of today's trade as part of global supply chains. Rather than a simple textbook two-country model, where final goods are traded and fully produced in one or the other country, today's trade is largely in intermediate goods where value chains can stretch over a number of countries, and domestic production may not necessarily imply domestic ownership. This complication has already become all too evident in the ongoing tarif dispute around the EU's import of 'Chinese' EVs, where it has turned out that European EV manufacturers often produce in China, while some 'European' EV manufacturers producing in Europe are actually owned by Chinese companies. The US is deeply integrated in these value chains, implying that the tarifs proposed by candidate Trump could have complex efects on activity and prices.

FIGURE 6. A relatively small share of goods the US imports are final consumption goods; goods imported by the US are extensively used as intermediate inputs in globally integrated production chains

## Complications II: Exchange rates, policy responses and confidence efects

Another conceptual challenge relates to potential policy reactions and exchange rate

responses. Tarifs on imports may lead to ofsetting appreciation of the domestic currency.<sup>10</sup> Specifically, an appreciation of the domestic currency makes imports cheaper and thus at least partly ofsets the efects of the tarif. In addition, while the tarif may discourage imports, the exchange rate appreciation may end up reducing exports too, with ultimately little change to the trade balance, unless the balance between saving and investment is changed.

Monetary policy can also play an important role. For example, if the inflationary impact of the tarif increase leads the central bank to keep tighter monetary policy than it would have otherwise, this would contribute to a further appreciation of the currency and a potentially deeper slowdown.

On the fiscal policy front, the use of the additional tarif revenues can play a role. The revenues from tarifs can in principle be redistributed to domestic households and firms, thereby contributing to stimulating domestic activity, especially if the revenue is used to reduce particularly distortive taxes.

Important negative efects can emanate from the general (trade) policy uncertainty and the related confidence loss (incl. financial stress) created by serious trade disputes. New tarifs create doubts about the viability of existing supply chains, while there is also no clarity on how to re-organise them. Often, an initial tarif increase is not the end of the story, retaliation measures follow, possibly leading to a tit-for-tat escalation. At the same time, it becomes less clear how monetary and fiscal policymakers may react. All of this lowers the confidence of domestic producers and consumers/workers, and can be compounded by financial market volatility.

## Quantifying the consequences of a '2025 trade war'

We consider the following scenario: Around mid-2025, the US imposes a 10% tarif on all goods imports from all countries, but a 60% tarif on goods imports from China. The afected trading partners retaliate fully and symmetrically. We quantify the efects on GDP and prices, relative to a baseline of no tarif change, for the US, euro area and China.

## Efects of higher import tarifs on activity

We compute the efects on GDP in three layers. First, we compute trade efects in the case that the US increases tarifs to 10% on imports from all trading partners, and the other countries retaliate symmetrically. Second, we compute the implications of another 50pp increase in the US tarif on imports from China (to bring the efective tarif to 60%), with China retaliating against the US. We then add the efects of the second layer to those of the first. Third, we add the efects on activity of trade policy uncertainty, given than they have been argued by researchers to play an important role in such a scenario, in part by shocking households, businesses and financial market participants' confidence.

## First layer: Raising import tarifs to 10%

In the first layer, given that the trade-weighted efective tarif rate on US imports is currently 2%, we assume that the US raises tarifs on imports by 8pp on all trading partners. We base our calculations on the IMF’s Global Integrated Monetary and Fiscal Model (GIMF),<sup>11</sup> in line with exercises performed in an ECB paper.<sup>12</sup> Faced with higher tarifs on (intermediate and final) imported goods, US consumers and firms re-direct their demand toward domestically produced goods. While this tends to stimulate US activity, this efect is more than ofset by the drop in aggregate demand due to 1) lower real income as a result of higher prices; and 2) reduced global demand for domestic exports, given the retaliatory tarifs imposed on US exports. In all, the model indicates that the net efect on US real GDP is negative, with the 10% import tarifs and retaliatory measures lowering real GDP by 1.2% in the first year.

While these model simulations provide a useful baseline, they likely exaggerate the adverse efect on the US economy for several reasons. 1) The model assumes that additional fiscal revenues from tarif increases are used to reduce deficits. More likely, these revenues would be recycled to support demand. We estimate that if the US government transfers the tax revenues from the tarif increase (around 0.9% of GDP) to households, it would raise GDP by 0.3%.<sup>13</sup> 2) The model assumes that monetary policy in each country reacts according to a simple Taylor-type

rule that adjusts the policy rate aggressively to current and 1-quarter-ahead inflation, among others. Such a policy rule implies an implausibly restrictive policy stance in the US, in our view. We estimate that a more realistic policy reaction function for the Fed would add 0.24% to the model-implied GDP growth in the first year. After incorporating those adjustments to both fiscal and monetary policy, we find that the net efect of 10% import tarifs and retaliatory measures would lower real GDP by 0.7% in the first year.

Turning to other regions, the EA sees its GDP increase. With all other countries raising tarifs on US exports, but not on one another's exports, EA producers see their competitiveness improve in non-US countries, thereby increasing their net exports. As a result, the model simulation implies that EA GDP would increase slightly (0.16%), even though the EA's tarifs on goods imported from the US contribute to slightly higher prices and thus a modest drop in real income. For China, we see only a negligible efect in this first simulation, since the tarif on US imports from China is currently 10% on a trade-weighted basis, and would thus remain unchanged. With US tarifs unchanged on imports from China, there would be, in turn, no reason for China to retaliate.

## Second layer: Raising US - China tarifs

In the second layer, we consider the output efects of an additional 50pp increase in the US tarif on imports from China to 60%, along with retaliation from China. We do so using simulations from the ECB’s global macroeconomic model.<sup>14</sup> The efect of these tarif increases between the US and China is contractionary for activity of both countries. The model implies that output drops by around 0.4% in the US on impact, while it contracts by 1.2% in China.<sup>15</sup>

By raising the costs of their imports, both countries see their real income decline, which exerts a drag on aggregate demand, even if some of the demand is shifting away from imports toward domestic production. For China, the output drop is more pronounced than in the US, in part because of trade diversion through other countries, which in turn gain trade shares. Moreover, while the model assumes that China stabilises the exchange rate versus a basket of currencies, US monetary policymakers, in contrast, initially raise rates slightly in response to the temporary increase in inflation, but then lower rates as activity weakens, thereby supporting consumption and investment.

Other countries sufer from the decline in global demand and overall activity, though some benefit from a partial ofset due to trade diversion. In the case of the EA, we find that GDP declines by 0.2%, dragged down by activity in both US and China.<sup>16</sup>

Adding the efects on GDP from the 10% universal tarif on imports and the 60% tarif on China, with full retaliation, we obtain an overall efect of -1.1% (= -0.7% - 0.4%) on US GDP and -1.2% on China's GDP. For the EA, the overall efect on GDP is slightly negative, at -0.1%, as the drag from global demand dominates the slight increase in activity resulting from improved competitiveness following the retaliation against the US's 10% universal tarif increase.

## Third layer: Efects of higher trade policy uncertainty on activity

Trade wars are often also associated with an increase in uncertainty (Figure 7). The increase in trade policy uncertainty (TPU) tends to have negative efects on growth and inflation above and beyond the efects mentioned above. Indeed, when uncertainty around the economic environment rises, firms typically hold of investment in new plants, new markets and new technologies, and also cut inventory investment. In addition, if higher uncertainty translates into higher risk premia and tighter financing and financial conditions, investment could decline because of the higher cost of capital. Lack of business investment growth in the UK post-Brexit referendum also provides evidence of the relevance of this channel. Hence, all else equal, the uncertainty channel is pointing in the direction of lower real GDP growth and inflation.

FIGURE 7. Trade policy uncertainty spiked during the first Trump administration
![](images/6328eec8876f52b70661074d347e786a1a0df82f84e30872f1ef63a6f0f63587.jpg)

The trade policy uncertainty index (TPU) is constructed by staf in the International Finance Division of the Federal Reserve Board and measures media attention to news related to trade policy uncertainty. See https://www.matteoiacoviello.com/ tpu.htm

Source: Caldara, Dario, Matteo Iacoviello, Patrick Molligo, Andrea Prestipino, and Andrea Rafo, "The Economic Efects of Trade Policy Uncertainty," manuscript presented at the 91st meeting of the Carnegie-Rochester-NYU Conference on Public Policy, April 2019.

To assess the efect of trade policy uncertainty, we estimate a simple vector autoregression (VAR) model that includes real GDP growth in the US, EA, and China, along with the TPU index, and estimate the response of real GDP in each region to an unexpected shock to TPU. Assuming that the new trade war would increase TPU by roughly the same extent as in early 2018, ahead of the first tarif imposed on China by the Trump administration, we find that GDP growth would be reduced by 0.3% in the US, 0.6% in the EA, and 0.8% in China.

In all, combining the efects from the tarif increases by the US along with other countries retaliation, and the added efects from the trade policy uncertainty, we obtain an overall efect of -1.4% on US GDP, -0.7% on Europe's GDP, and -2.0% on China's activity.

FIGURE 8. Tarif increases with retaliation would likely reduce activity in the US, EA and China
![](images/9dfa6596d47bf340eadc4985116d8e8701878bd2dc913ed4c4a6acffbd1e4c5e.jpg)
Numbers may not add due to rounding. Source: Barclays Research

## Efects of higher tarifs on prices

## US inflation

To assess the efect of tarif increases on prices in the scenario considered, we first attempt to quantify the maximum direct impact on domestic inflation of higher import prices, and then adjust this estimate for other macroeconomic factors. As before, we decompose the analysis into two parts, first by considering the efects of a 10% universal tarif on US imports, and second by adding the efects of raising tarifs on imports from China to 60%.

## Estimating maximum direct impact on US prices

In the first layer, we estimate that raising the universal tarif from 2% to 10%, an 8pp increase, could boost inflation by 80bp, assuming full pass-through. This is because, as research from the San Francisco Fed has estimated, import shares in the CPI basket are rather modest, at around 10%, after adjusting for the import content of domestically produced goods. Moreover, since the current trade-weighted tarif on all Chinese imports, including those that currently do not face any tarifs, is about 10%, the average tarif on US imports from China is essentially unchanged in that first step.

In the second layer, a 60% tarif on all imports from China would mean that those currently under no tarifs and existing tarifs of 25% and 7.5% under Lists 1, 2, 3 and 4A, are raised to 60%. In the event, the trade-weighted tarif on imports from China would jump by 50pp, to 60%. Using the import share of China in the US consumption basket, which is around 2%, we estimate that such a tarif policy could boost US inflation by 100bp, assuming full pass-through.

Combining the two layers, we obtain the direct maximal impact on US prices from the 10% across-the-board import tarif, and a 60% tarif on China. This implies CPI increase of 1.80pp, under full pass through.

## Weighing arguments for only partial pass-through

Of course, a full pass-through is unlikely for several reasons, including swift substitution efects, like what happened post the previous trade war (see Global Trade: Back to the bad old days? and Protectionism to intensify cost pressures in tradable goods sectors). As shown in Figure 9, the volumes of Chinese imports hit with 25% tarifs dropped of swiftly, just a few months after the tarifs were announced, with imports of these same products from the rest of the world (ROW)

picking up. Indeed, as evidence of trade diversion, after 2017, China has tended to increase exports to countries such as Mexico and Vietnam, from where the US has increased its imports.

In addition, the trade partners' retaliation, which would lower the demand for US exports, and lower US output as discussed above, would likely force US producers to compress their margins and lower their price increases, in line with traditional Phillips curves. In all, we estimate that the combination of the substitution efects and the macroeconomic efects from the drag on activity would reduce the overall price impact by half of the full pass-through. As a result, we would expect the import tarif increase with retaliation to increase the CPI by about 0.9pp. FIGURE 9. Imports from China hit by tarifs have fallen sharply, while imports from RoW have picked up

![](images/72cbac71ba9bae3effe91dfe7e6b562c5076883524a88a0e49c694832f669a32.jpg)
Data updated through May 2024 Source: USITC, Barclays Research

## Euro area inflation

In the euro area, the weighted-average tarif on imports from the US is currently around 1%, implying an increase of c.9pp to 10% in the event of full retaliation to Trump's proposed trade measures. Meanwhile, we estimate the share of the EA inflation basket subject to tarifs (1.4%) by multiplying the share of imports in total final consumption goods (12%) by the share of US products in total imports (11.4%). Combining the potential tarif increase with the share of CPI products subject to tarifs implies that the direct efect on CPI could be around 0.1pp. However, the small decline in EA output we expect in 2025 is likely to mitigate any price increases and firms' pricing power. Thus, we estimate the efect of a renewed trade war on euro area CPI to be less than 0.1pp.

## How would central banks respond?

In the face of tarif increases, the central banks' monetary policy response is a priori unclear. The Fed would likely face a significant trade-of between the two sides of its dual mandate, with inflation increasing by nearly 1pp and activity falling 1.4% compared with the no-tarif baseline, which would result in a weaker labour market. We believe that the tarif increase would have a fairly rapid but temporary impact on inflation, while the drag on activity would be more gradual and persistent, lasting more than a year. In that case, we would expect the Fed to first raise rates relative to our no-tarif baseline, responding only partially to the rise in the inflation rate, as it would expect higher inflation to be temporary, but then to cut rates more aggressively as inflation peaks and a weakening in activity materializes.

More specifically, we would expect the policy rate to be initially raised about 25-50bp above our baseline level in response to more elevated inflation, but then to be lowered and reaching about 100bp below our baseline in the second year, as activity remained weak. Starting from a baseline case in which the FOMC is gradually cutting rates through much of 2025, our estimates for the impact of tarif increases imply that the FOMC would initially cut rates by 25-50bp less than in our baseline, but then cut more aggressively by the end of 2025 and 2026.

For the ECB, we would expect no change to the policy rate path initially because we would expect a more muted response of inflation. However, as the negative impact on activity from the tarif war becomes apparent, we think the ECB would likely cut rates by an additional 50bp compared with our current forecast.

In China, in response to a larger decline in GDP growth and deflationary efects from any tarif war, we think the PBoC would deploy monetary policy easing tools more aggressively to arrest a growth slowdown and fight deflation. We would expect the central bank to lower policy rates by about 20-40bp more and cut RRR by 100-200bp more than in our baseline forecasts.

## Box: A bottom-up robustness check for China

We complement our analysis and check the robustness of our conclusions for China through a bottom-up analysis by our China economics team. Estimating the direct efects on net

exports and indirect efects on consumption and investment, using elasticities from the 2018-19 experience, they arrive at an aggregate drag on real GDP growth of around \~2pp over a 12-month horizon, in line with our main results.

Direct efect via net exports: Taking into account the tarif elasticity, the Sino-US trade value, trade diversion, and the value-added ratio, we estimate Trump's proposed tarifs on all Chinese goods would result in a \~5pp decline in China's total export growth, and reduce China's GDP growth by \~1pp. The risks to these estimates could be skewed to the downside: for example, export elasticity to tarifs of such magnitude could turn out to be nonlinear; the US could tighten loopholes on trade diversion, which dampened the efect of the 2018-19 tarifs; market scale in non-US destinations may be insuficient, and more Chinese exporters might exit the market due to low profit margins.

Indirect efect through investment and consumption: China’s manufacturing sector is highly export-intensive, which resulted in a sizeable hit to manufacturing investment during the 2018-19 trade war episode. With value-added exports accounting for \~50% of total manufacturing valued added in 2023, we estimate that the proposed sharp tarif increases under a Trump administration in the US could translate into a 2.5pp decline in manufacturing investment, or a \~0.5pp decline in GDP growth. We estimate that the hit to economic growth from weakening consumption would amount to another 0.5pp, through declining wage growth in various export-related sectors, deteriorating sentiment and negative wealth efect from falling asset prices.

Efect on inflation: Chinese consumer goods are mostly supplied by domestic manufacturers and China’s imports from the US only account for c.6% of its total imports and 0.8% of its GDP. The transmission channels from tarif increases would be three-fold: 1) exchange rate depreciation; 2) potential retaliatory tarif hikes; and 3) weaker GDP growth. Overall, we think a new trade war (assuming China’s enacts retaliatory tarif measures) would, on balance, have a deflationary efect, and we estimate the net efect would show a 0.1% decline in inflation in the first year. Such estimates take into account 1) a 0.4% increase in CPI inflation from higher imported prices due to China's tarif retaliation and CNY depreciation, and 2) a 0.5% decrease in CPI inflation due to weakening demand and intensifying price competition.

## Conclusion

The trade policy proposals from the Trump team imply a potentially radical change in the US's approach to free trade that would efectively reverse decades of global trade liberalisation. Our analysis indicates that the economic efects would be more consequential than those stemming from the limited trade war with China in 2018-19. Our estimations show large output losses for all involved, but especially for the US and China (assuming a 60% tarif on both sides, as China retaliates symmetrically). This is an important finding, as it undermines the notion that there could be winners in such a trade war.

Some may hope that any trade policies ultimately implemented in a second Trump administration would unlikely be as extreme as those currently proposed. Our Washington Research analyst notes that the former president sees himself as a dealmaker and, as such, the threat of tarifs provides negotiating leverage. Thus, the purpose of Trump's tarif threats may be to bring China to the negotiating table rather than any intention to immediately implement them as proposed. Moreover, in the 2018-19 trade war, Trump used Section 201, 232, and 301 tarifs, which allow the president to impose tarifs on specific goods for specific reasons, eg, national security. In contrast, the legal basis for the imposition of universal tarifs without the approval of Congress is less clear.

A final point can be made that the assumed symmetric response by trade partners through retaliatory tarifs on goods imports seems unlikely. For example, other countries could specifically target certain important goods (like China's targeting of Harley Davidson motorbikes or French brandy) or they could impose other trade restrictions, eg, China restricting exports of certain rare metals.

However, a related risk would be any tarif tit-for-tat would not stop after the first round. In addition, if Chinese exporters, efectively banned from the US market through the 60% tarif, sought new destinations, other countries could raise their tarifs on Chinese imports. In such a scenario, a potential upward spiral in tarifs could severely disrupt global trade flows.

In all, real-world efects on trade, activity, and inflation are likely to be more complex than envisaged in this exercise.

Global Trade

# Tarifs: Cross-asset implications

Given the combination of higher prices and lower growth shocks that tarifs tend to imply, the growth efects are likely ultimately to lead the diferentiating impulse response across assets. We identify and quantify the efects on global equities, credit, the yield curve and the dollar.

• The adverse growth impulse of horizontal tarifs would likely weigh on global long-term yields. In the US, the very front end would likely sell of and 2s10s yield curve flatten as nearterm price spikes delay Fed cuts. In the euro area, the negative impulse would likely dominate across the curve.

• The dollar would likely benefit, particularly versus currencies of economies running large surpluses versus the US. We estimate a direct boost to the dollar of at least  3-4%  (assuming large elasticities of import substitution). Confidence efects could amplify this (retaliation would likely partially ofset it).

• For US equities, we estimate that Trump's proposed tarifs would create a 3.2% drag on 2025 SPX EPS, with an additional 1.5% hit from retaliatory measures. The materials, discretionary, industrials, technology and healthcare sectors are likely most at risk.

• A  full blown tarif war with retaliations can lead to a single-digit drag on European EPS growth, we estimate. Germany and Italy look to be most at risk, as well as sectors like capital goods, autos, beverages and chemicals.

• For US credit, the IG pharmaceuticals, aerospace & defense, building materials, and retail sectors are highly exposed to tarif escalations, but are not currently pricing in much risk. In Europe, IG credit might initially struggle with higher rates volatility, but a near-textbook growth shock would likely imply HY underperformance, alongside that of cyclicals and Autos across IG and HY.

# Public Policy: How quickly could tarifs be efectuated?

Michael McLean<sup>(v)</sup> BCI, US

Congress has delegated broad authority to the president to set tarifs under various laws. The process and timeline by which this happens would depend on which legal authority the president uses. Some of the statutory authorities could allow a president to impose tarifs quickly (though these tend to be recently untested with a higher risk of legal challenges), while other authorities require agencies (the Commerce Department, US Trade Representative, or International Trade Commission) to conduct investigations first, which would take more time but may be more legally durable. Federal courts have upheld the constitutionality of Congress's delegation of tarif authority to the executive branch, and tarifs have survived legal challenges.

## What's past is prologue

We look to former President Trump's first term as a precedent. In his first term, he imposed tarifs using three laws: Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962 (for steel and aluminum), Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974 (for US imports from China), and Section 201 of the Trade Act of 1974 (for washing machines and solar cells/modules).<sup>17</sup>

## Section 232

The Section 232 tarifs took 11 months to implement from the start of the Commerce Department's initiation of its investigation.

• April 2017: Commerce initiates investigations on US steel and aluminum imports

• January 2018: Commerce submits reports to the president

• March 2018: The president imposes tarifs

## Section 201

The Section 201 tarifs took nine months after the initiation of the International Trade Commission's (ITC) investigations.

• May 2017: US industry petition initiates ITC's investigation of solar cells/modules

• June 2017: US industry petition initiates ITC's investigation of residential washers

• September/October 2017: ITC makes afirmative injury determinations

• November/December 2017: ITC submits report with recommended tarifs to the president

• January 2018: The president announces tarifs

• February 2018: Tarifs take efect

## Section 301

The Trump administration used Section 301 to impose four consecutive stages of tarifs on about two-thirds of US imports from China. Stage 1 tarifs were imposed 11 months after he directed the US Trade Representative (USTR) to investigate perceived unfair trade practices. Subsequent stages were able to be imposed more quickly because the investigation had already been completed. Stage 2 tarifs were imposed a month and a half after stage 1. Stage 3 tarifs were imposed one month after stage 2, and stage 4 tarifs were imposed about a year after stage 3.

• August 2017: The president directs the USTR to investigate China trade unfair practices • March 2018: The USTR releases Section 301 report; the president directs the USTR to impose tarifs

• July 2018: Stage 1 tarifs take efect

• August 2018: Stage 2 tarifs take efect

• September 2018: Stage 3 tarifs take efect

• December 2018: The president announces negotiations with China to resolve US concerns

• May 2019: Stage 3 tarifs increase takes efect

• September 2019: Stage 4 tarifs take efect

## How might a second Trump administration be diferent?

## What could Trump do on day one?

President Trump could invoke two infrequently used laws that may allow him to efectuate broad, unilateral tarifs quickly. These actions could be more controversial and susceptible to legal challenges because of the lack of precedent for their use to set tarifs.

## International Emergency Economic Powers Act (IEEPA)

In our view, the most drastic, day one action Trump could take would be to invoke the IEEPA, which provides the president with extraordinary authority to regulate all international commerce in response to a declared national emergency (pursuant to the National Emergencies Act) for "unusual or extraordinary" threats to national security, foreign policy, or the economy. While presidents have used the IEEPA to implement embargoes, sanctions, and asset freezes, none has used it to impose tarifs.

On May 30, 2019, former President Trump announced his intention to use the IEEPA to impose a 5% tarif on all goods imported from Mexico starting on June 10, 2019, citing a national emergency at the US southern border. On June 7, he reversed his intention and the tarifs did not take efect. Since they were never imposed, the president's authority to use IEEPA in this way was not litigated. An open question is whether courts, which generally have given substantial deference to the president in national security, would uphold this use of the IEEPA or issue an injunction to at least temporarily stop the tarifs from going into efect.

For example, IEEPA could be used to impose tarifs on China by declaring China a "unusual and extraordinary threat." It could be far-reaching to justify broad-based tarifs using IEEPA under a national emergency that would cover allies including Europe, Canada, or Mexico, for example. A national emergency is easy for a president to declare by issuing an executive order. Congress can pass a joint resolution of disapproval to terminate a national emergency, but a veto-proof supermajority would be needed.

There is some precedent. In 1971, President Nixon declared a national emergency and imposed a 10% tarif on all imports into the United States, citing his authority under the IEEPA’s precursor statute, the Trading with the Enemy Act of 1917 (TWEA), Section 338 of the Tarifs Act of 1930, and Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962.<sup>18</sup> Nixon issued the proclamation on August 15, 1971, and the tarifs went into efect the next day.

## Section 122 temporary tarifs

Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 authorizes a president to deal with “large and serious United States balance-of-payments” deficits by imposing temporary (150 days, unless extended by Congress) import surcharges on any goods, not exceeding 15%. Similar to the IEEPA, Section 122 does not require an prerequisite investigation, which could allow the president to act more quickly. Imports from countries with significant current account surpluses (such as China) could be targets for any such measures, though it is not clear if a balance of payments problem exists. There could be legal challenges over what constitutes a "large and serious balance of payment deficit."

## First 100 days: Fast-tracked Section 301 tarifs on China

The president could adjust the Section 301 tarifs on China, including increasing and broadening them, without needing the USTR to initiate a new investigation. Therefore, changes to the Section 301 tarifs on China could be efectuated much more quickly (in a few months) than previously, supported by the prior investigations (including most recently the Biden administration's four-year review) that found unfair trade practices regarding technology transfer, intellectual property, and innovation. Using Section 301 to impose new tarifs on goods from countries other than China would require the USTR to initiate a new investigation and subsequent opportunity for notice and public comment.

## Other longer-term authorities

## Section 338 of the Tarif Act of 1930

Trump could also use Section 338, which permits the president to impose tarifs of up to 50% on imports from countries that have "discriminated" against US commerce compared with another foreign country. Further, it permits the president to block imports from a certain country entirely. An ITC investigation is a prerequisite, so this likely would take months, and the outcome would not be guaranteed. The ITC is an independent agency and generally less susceptible to political pressure, though the president can influence it through appointing likeminded commissioners (subject to Senate confirmation) as vacancies arise. Section 338 has never been used to impose tarifs on imports from a foreign country and has not been utilized since 1949. The outcome of a Section 338 investigation, including the credibility of fact patterns, could get litigated in court.

## Advance legislation in Congress

Finally, a president could work with Congress to advance tarifs or other trade-related policy in legislation. Figure 2 lists three possible legislative actions on trade. Advancing tarif legislation in Congress likely would be the slowest route.

FIGURE 1. Possible Unilateral Presidential Actions on Trade under US Law

# Global Rates: The "stag" in "stagflation" would win out

Anshul Pradhan<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US  |  Rohan Khanna<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK

Across-the-board tarifs are likely to result in a significantly adverse global growth shock, with heightened trade uncertainty. Absent a material ofset from easier fiscal policy, we believe longterm yields would move lower as a result. The US would likely face a significant inflationary impulse at the same time, which would lead the very front end to sell of as Fed cuts are delayed. This would result in a flatter 2s10s yield curve, posing a threat to popular curvesteepener trades, in our view. In the euro area, the negative growth impulse would dominate across the curve.

## US Rates: Reshaping the curve

As noted in the earlier chapter "A new trade war: Counting the costs", across-the-board tarifs imposed by the US on trading partners would create a material stagflationary impulse. In the scenario of 60% and 10% tarifs on goods imports from China and the RoW, respectively, US inflation would be 0.9pp higher, and assuming heightened trade uncertainty amid full retaliation from trading partners, US real GDP would be 1.4pp lower (both versus the baseline). This poses a conundrum for rates investors; should they respond to higher inflation by pushing yields higher or to lower growth by pushing yields lower?

We believe the very front end would sell of, as the Fed would slow the easing campaign, which, in light of aggressive cuts priced in over 2025, would result in higher front-end rates. However, further out the curve, we believe the "stag" in "stagflation" would win out, with the markets ultimately pricing in more cuts cumulatively, resulting in lower long-term rates. This would be exacerbated by a bullish impulse from rest of the world, which would primarily face an adverse growth shock. A potential ofset would be the fiscal response in the US, and the make up of the Congress would be key to watch.

## A couple of things of note:

First, markets appear to be pricing in a very low likelihood of an aggressive price shock. Figure 3 shows that 1yf 1y inflation CPI swaps are trading at just 2.2%, implying PCE inflation slightly below the Fed's 2% target. Further out the curve, 5y5y inflation swaps are also trading at the recent lows. One could argue that these are being weighed down by the tail risk of a recession and the resulting very low inflation in that scenario; a read across asset classes suggests that recession risks are still muted (for example, credit spreads are quite tight). In our view, low inflation swap rates reflect a benign baseline view and reduced worries about upside inflation risks, which is also why 5y5y inflation swap rates are low. Our economists estimate that across-the-board tarifs would raise 1y-ahead CPI inflation 0.85pp, pushing it well above market pricing.

Second, given low worries about rising inflation, markets are putting a very low weight on the Fed's keeping the policy rate elevated; doing so would come as a meaningful surprise. Figure 4 shows the option market-implied distribution of the policy rate by YE 25. As can be seen, the modal outlook is for the Fed to cut to about 3%. The likelihood of very aggressive cuts is not unusually high (15% probability of the policy rate <1.5%), which is consistent with recession risks not being seen as unusually elevated. On the flip side, there is about a 20% probability of the policy rate being >4% by YE 25. Hence, even if the Fed does not hike in response to higher inflation, pausing the easing campaign (which, in our view, is very plausible in a trade war) is also seen as a low likelihood scenario.

Hence, the market is of the view that high inflation is not a concern any more and the Fed can ease aggressively over the coming year. A trade war would likely change that picture.

Some have argued that conduct of monetary policy might change under President Trump and the Fed would set policy on a dovish path, mitigating its efect on the policy rate. For instance, during his administration, President Trump repeatedly called for the Fed to lower rates. Still, he has also noted that he would simply not reappoint (rather than try to fire) Powell as chair of the Federal Reserve Board after his term expires (in May 2026).<sup>19</sup>  Further, we are skeptical that the next Fed chair would deviate from setting monetary policy with respect to its dual mandate. For instance, in 2020, the Senate voted not to confirm the nomination of Judy Shelton, who was widely seen as an unorthodox candidate, to the Federal Reserve Board.<sup>20</sup>

FIGURE 3. Inflation swaps are trading at the low end of the range
![](images/eb372384ccf90e5ac6a8057a976d5405749ee9f5f7688a5b152885ec629800f1.jpg)
Source: Barclays Research

FIGURE 4. Markets are putting a low weight on the Fed's pausing the easing cycle in 2025
Prob. of 1M SOFR in a certain range, %
![](images/35a97db60cc0f908d5cfd91d4a075a098e4fc53c4a6b2a1c9f3401af8697ffa5.jpg)
Source: Barclays Research

Further out the curve, the dynamics would be diferent. The key drivers for long-term rates would be a reassessment of the the trough of the easing cycle and term premium. Both would argue for lower long-term rates.

Ultimately, the price shock should prove temporary and below trend growth create slack in the labor market and pull inflation forecasts lower (versus the prior baseline). In a typical policy rule, that would argue for the Fed eventually to bring the policy rate below the prior baseline. At the end of the day, global trade restrictions would likely be detrimental to potential growth; as a result, all else equal, one should also expect the neutral rate to be lower. Figure 5 shows that as the markets have priced in a lower trough of the easing cycle, long-term rates have also come down.

Further, in the rest of the word, the impulse is tilted even more towards an adverse growth shock. For instance, in the euro area, our economists expect inflation to rise a tenth, but growth to fall 0.7pp (China growth would also be marked down substantially). This would be bullish for global bonds (see below for the efect on European long-term rates), which would reduce the term premium in the US as well. For instance, if Bunds meaningfully break below 2%, that would pull US long-term yields lower as well.

Hence, across-the-board tarifs would be bullish for long-term rates. Overall, the 2s10s yield curve is likely to flatten, as the front end would need to reprice to higher inflation and a delayed easing cycle and the long end to lower growth prospects.

A key question for rates investors would be the overall stance of fiscal policy amid an ongoing trade war. A budget deficit-neutral trade policy, ie, tarif revenues being used to fund tax cuts and provide government subsidies, would mitigate the growth efect; however it would still be growth negative, as heightened uncertainty would weigh on investments. However, a material expansion of deficits could potentially more than ofset the adverse growth efect and tilt the impulse to higher inflation, rather than lower growth. Hence, the makeup of Congress would be key. For instance, in a unified Republican government, rates could move higher across the curve. Figure 6 shows that the election markets have put a relatively low likelihood of R sweep; such an outcome would come as a surprise to the markets.

FIGURE 5. As the trough of the easing cycle has come down, so have long-term rates
![](images/facfc0997038ee539c1eb5715ac2097de46d034616d8174c349bcfd2d7c17514.jpg)
Source: Barclays Research

FIGURE 6. A Republican sweep could potentially ofset the drag from a trade war
![](images/796bc57fa9c3feab2246a85b892aabf5fd1354aad38f92add0069348a25fc033.jpg)
Source: Polymarket, Barclays Research

## EUR Rates: Confidence hit to rally Bunds

For the past year and a half, our central thesis on Bunds has been that they are in the passenger seat of global fixed income. Specifically, caught in a tug-of-war between sizable foreign shocks and domestic fundamentals, Bunds have been trading in at 2-3% yields. If term premium shifts were the primary driver of Bunds in 2023, the evolution of rate expectations has been the primary driver this year (Figure 7).

In recent months, pessimism about Europe's growth prospects has grown, given that the manufacturing sector remains in doldrums and slack is building in EA labour markets. Even though headline growth numbers are better than last year's, the underlying picture is weak, with a consumption bounce from positive real wage growth not coming through yet. In this already weak setting, the imposition of tarifs could turn out to be a body blow to already fragile market sentiment on Europe (Figure 8).

FIGURE 7. Changes in rate expectations have been the primary driver of  10y Bunds in recent months
![](images/c061eaafa9bd780b7e4727595e4989a79a3687f13e7bbfae801f49cf8b089478.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 8. Bunds are being pulled down by a rethink of nominal growth expectations; a tarif shock would only make this worse
![](images/110762c6dd6d6aae9a6b3b744cbd74753678093dc64d528b82014b42bcebf16f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Indeed, as our economists have shown in the previous chapter, the trade war headwind for Europe is likely to manifest more powerfully through the confidence channel, on account of higher policy uncertainty, rather than the actual trade channel. They see negative and longlasting efects of these policies on euro area real GDP, but relatively muted efects on inflation. Calibrating the shock to the scale of the 2017-18 trump tarifs, our economists pencil in a potential 1% decline in euro area real GDP and only a 0.2% increase in the headline price level. Naturally, relative to their baseline view, which is for the ECB’s terminal rate reaching 2.5%, they see more easing of 50bp, on account of this negative growth outcome.

All else equal, these dynamics would create a bullish backdrop for European rates and may finally be able to move them into the driving seat. In Breaking Bunds (below 2%), we had argued that to generate a sustained rally, market pricing efectively needs to shift towards a hard-landing. From a domestic perspective, the necessary conditions include a downside revision in the underlying inflation profile or a significant surprise to the ECB's growth and inflation projections (Figure 9). Alternatively, Bunds will need to rely on the kindness of strangers—ie, external spillovers—to help them glide below 2% (Figure 10).

What the tarif shock, as calibrated by our economics team, does is generate bullish impulses from domestic and external spillovers for EUR rates. With the hit to growth, the ECB would likely look through the small uptick in inflation and instead argue that the weakened demand outlook and uncertainty shock would ensure that any increase in inflation is not persistent; hence, monetary policy can respond to ofset the weakness in demand. In the US, as argued above, the bulk of the pressure higher would be in the front end of the curve, so the long end would be unlikely to generate strong bearish spillovers. In summary, this outcome should allow for Bunds to break well below 2%.

FIGURE 9. With pricing of a faster and deeper ECB cutting cycle, 10y Bunds would likely slip below 2%
![](images/275def6f951048fffefb86b500260cc3274387743ee964398aacf9ddebded4e8.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 10. Given the strong influence of US yields on other markets, a bullish dynamic in US yields would also help Bunds break below 2%

![](images/5c6e141a7794475686bc293d1f91c7d947399e3e85ddaaf665886244d41ecaea.jpg)

Chart shows beta of 10y bond yields to a 100bp rise in US 10y. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

# The FX impact of Trump's tarifs proposal

Themistoklis Fiotakis<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK  |  Lefteris Farmakis<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK

The imposition of yet more tarifs – potentially also against long-standing US trading partners and not just China – is a key policy proposal that Trump already used during his first term.

As we discussed in our March Global Outlook, in general, US tarifs tend to support the dollar via the import substitution channel, even as the size of the FX impact depends crucially on the details. We expect dollar gains to be larger when tarifs apply to economies with large trade surpluses versus the US (eg, China) and somewhat muted when trade is more balanced (eg, versus the eurozone). An all-out trade war against all trading partners, on the other hand, could end up weighing on the dollar via retaliation. Broader confidence efects should also be considered; in general, they tend to amplify the dollar-supportive first-round impact.

Figure 11 contains an estimate of the FX efect of key trade policy pronouncements by Trump, based on the model contained in Jeanne and Son (2023). Under this approach, the total FX NEER response to the imposition of tarifs by the US on a foreign country is a function of a) the estimated "FX ofsets", namely the fraction of the tarif ofset by FX moves both for the original US tarif and the foreign ones imposed via retaliation, and b) the share of foreign imports on which the tarif is applied (and ditto for exports into the foreign country in question that retaliates).

We stress-test four scenarios for their FX implications, roughly corresponding to key trade policy pronouncements by Donald Trump: a) one where the US imposes a blanket tarif of 10% on all goods imports without retaliation, b) one where this blanket tarif is met with partial retaliation from trading partners, c) one where the US imposes a tarif of 10% on all eurozone goods imports with full retaliation, and d) one where the US imposes a 60% tarif on all Chinese goods imports, also with full retaliation.

FIGURE 11. Simulated impact of tarifs on FX under diferent scenarios
<table><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=5>Scenario                                      Impact on FX NEER</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1></td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1></td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Min</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Max</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>Average</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>US imposes a 10% tariff worldwide withoutretaliation</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>USD</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>3.0</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>3.8</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>3.4</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>US imposes a 10% tariff worldwide withpartial retaliation</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>USD</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>1.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>0.3</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=2 colspan=1>US imposes a 60% tariff on China importswith retaliation</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>USD</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.9</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>CNY</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-2.5</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-3.7</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-3.1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=2 colspan=1>US imposes a 10% tariff on EA imports withretaliation</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>USD</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.7</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-1.3</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-1.0</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>EUR</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.9</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-1.4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-1.1</td></tr></table>

Note: Partial retaliation amounts to an efective tarif on all US imports of 3%. We also account for existing China tarifs in our estimates. Diferent specifications allow for Producer Currency Pricing, Dominant Currency Pricing and diferent import elasticities. For details on the full methodology, see Jeanne and Son (2023).
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

In the cleanest case, a blanket 10% tarif on all US goods imports that goes unanswered boosts the dollar efective exchange rate by about 3 % via this mechanism alone. We should note here that the assumed import elasticities in this model are rather large - smaller elasticities would imply that the risk to this 3% dollar strength are skewed to the upside.

It would, take only partial retaliation from trading partners to ofset this impact, as the share of global exports to the US is quite small (while the totality of US imports and exports is now subject to tarifs).

Bilateral tarifs, on the other hand, have diferential results. US trade with the eurozone is more or less symmetric; therefore, the efects of tarifs efectively cancel each other out. US exports to China, on the other hand, are a much smaller share of US total exports relative to Chinese exports to the US. This results in much larger damage for the renminbi (of 3-4%) in our estimates.

In addition to the direct impact of tarifs on FX via the import substitution channel, confidence efects also play a crucial role. One can see in Figure 12 evidence of this channel of transmission in the substantial drop in sentiment indices (eg, PMIs) for large, open economies, such as China and the eurozone, since the announcement of the first round of tarifs by President Trump in January 2018. Although other factors were also at play, notably China's own deleveraging eforts at the time, confidence efects likely contributed to the deceleration in global growth trends between 2018 and COVID.

FIGURE 12. Tarifs also work via confidence efects
![](images/22c91fea60672f9d2a513e06274944d99e533f73ad1e0741b7691c05ebae3fc8.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 13. The US-ROW potential growth gap widened
![](images/ba9b35999de160d7deb5c6e3e3bfd619a0d1734ddb8f94626e735d07c17be22c.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, OECD, Barclays Research

What is more, such efects do not appear irrational or unfounded; instead, they are in line with ample evidence that trade restrictions weigh negatively on output and productivity (eg, see this IMF report on the Macroeconomic Consequences of Tarifs). Remarkably, the potential growthrate gap between the US and other OECD members opened up during that time (Figure 13). Accordingly, the dollar's broad-based appreciation at that time can be attributed predominantly to weaker global growth trends. Confidence efects, in turn, likely contributed more to its outperformance than the more immediate import substitution channel.

## Global Credit

Dominique Toublan<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US  |  Robert Zauner<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US  |  Soren Willemann<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK  | Zoso Davies<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK  |  Craig Nicol<sup>(i)</sup> Barclays, UK  |  Andrew Keches, CFA<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US  | Priya Ohri-Gupta, CFA<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US  |  Afsah Quadri<sup>(i)</sup> BCI, US

## US Credit: Avoiding the potholes

Credit investors are focusing on tarifs as a key US election risk, as the president has broad unilateral authority to set them. We believe that the three main market drivers related to the election are tarifs, taxes and regulations, as we discussed in Pricing the US election in the credit markets. The biggest tarif concerns can pose a material headwind to credit markets, especially when coupled with other underlying macro worries. For instance, the efect of the China trade tensions in 2018-19 was exacerbated by worries about growth and Fed policy errors (Figure 14).

We find that the pharmaceutical, aerospace and defense, building materials, and retail sectors are most exposed to a tarif spike. However, they are not pricing in much of that risk at current spread levels (Figure 17). These sector-level conclusions are based on a bottom-up approach to identify IG companies that are most exposed to tarif risk. This identifies issuers that would sufer the most in a higher tarif environment, combined with rising trade tensions because of their revenue streams and/or the global nature of their supply chains. See Figure 18 for a list of most exposed tickers.

Trade tensions with China, combined with previous economic uncertainty, resulted in a weak 2018 for US credit markets

Credit spreads widened in the early stages of the 2018-19 China trade war, especially on the IG side. Figure 14 shows investment grade and high yield spreads with a timeline of key trade war events (event details are listed in Figure 19).

At first, IG drifted wider vs. HY, by 22bp between the first event in early March to July, while HY spreads were more range bound, essentially trading between 314bp and 372bp, with no marked trend. While the more globally exposed nature of the IG index could have exacerbated investor concerns, the outperformance of lower-rated credit could be partly explained by the increase in risk-free rates, with wider-trading debt better able to absorb a rise in rates over that period (see here). Spreads were not overly reactive to any single headline, but they did react to escalating trade tensions. These were not the only headwinds at the time, as the Fed was also hiking and overall economic concerns were rising, especially towards the end of the year.

Concerns about trade tensions may not explicitly dictate spreads moves, but they can exacerbate them if there are other macro concerns. Spreads had a sharp period of widening in IG and HY at the end of 2018 (Figure 14), as worries about a Fed policy error started to take hold. Data point #10 in the figure marks the start of the 90-day halt in US-China trade escalations. While this could be interpreted as a tension-cooling development, markets expressed ongoing concern about uncertainties over this period, given the unpredictability of actions during the trade war. This shows that the combination of trade uncertainty and other macro worries is likely to amplify investor concerns, a key observation to keep in mind when positioning around the election.

FIGURE 14. Investment grade and high yield spread moves during the 2018-19 China trade war; see Figure 19 for details about each event
![](images/5f7102eb3c3fc07f9875f9e096c1c1f67af7b46e9ca0c84850106a496098de51.jpg)
Escalating events: considered to be those that would intensify trade tensions (eg,. new tarif announcement/proposal) Easing events: considered to be those that would cool trade tensions (eg, US-China trade negotiations resuming) Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Single-company risk is more salient than sector risk if a future tarif war follows the 2018-19 template

Despite mounting trade tensions, not all traditionally tarif-exposed sectors priced in the risks in 2018-19. Figure 15 shows the spread moves of IG sectors with higher exposure to increased tarifs vs. the IG index. Some, such as retailers, consumer products, and technology, did not show signs of material weakness at the sector level, even though much of the index had some reliance on global supply chains.

However, looking under the surface, tarif-related underperformance was more salient at the individual company level. Figure 16 highlights three consumer product/retail tickers that experienced spread weakness as a results of increased trade tensions throughout 2018. DLTR, DG and HAS began to underperform starting in the beginning of April 2018 as trade tensions heated up and markets priced in concerns about their supply chain exposures.

Later that year, these tickers underperformed sharply as trade uncertainties remained topical and pressure from weak fundamentals and macro worries eroded investor sentiment. This underscores the importance of identifying the companies with heavy exposure to international supply chains when navigating dynamic tarif regimes.

FIGURE 15. IG sector spread moves over China trade war period
![](images/36ca6334efcbc2b8cdd30e3447050e188e610096d7e79714dc4a02a378e1e561.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 16. Underperformance from tarif exposed tickers during China trade war
![](images/2254a68fc44d564ada84ef4f4e04eb1d4157bedd40b6c5ddde59f0bf64590dbf.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## Sectors at risk of tarif escalation this time

The pharmaceutical, aerospace and defense, building materials and retail sectors have the largest concentration of issuers most exposed to a tarif hike. Figure 17 aggregates the tickers included in our screen at the sector level to visualize where there are more companies with tarif-related risks.

However, these sectors are trading pretty tight and not pricing in much tarif risk: they trade rich to the IG index after adjusting for rating, duration, ratings, price (among other variables). Investors looking to identity risks should focus on the sectors and tickers in Figure 17 and Figure 18.

FIGURE 17. Pharma, A&D, Building Mat, and Retailers are most exposed to tarifs; however, they are trading rich to the IG index and do not seem to be pricing much of the risk
![](images/bb6488a94315a3b704c148cb3b16833bedc444928b8bf2b66af9b79afbbe2c1e.jpg)
Sectors shown are only those that had tickers in our tarif screen. The x-axis represents the amount outstanding for each ticker included as a percent of the total sector amount. The y-axis uses our quantitative relative value method; see Sector relative value for more details. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Pharmaceuticals: The risks of tarifs could be a headwind to the pharmaceutical industry, given the exposure to China. Some of the risk could be modestly ofset by significant overseas manufacturing capacity, but not enough to protect companies from the entire efect. Retaliatory tarifs are a bigger risk and more material than in the past, as the Chinese

pharmaceutical industry has matured and ofers some substitute products. Tarifs could also drive some switching from American-sourced products to European manufacturers.

Aerospace & Defense: The global nature of the aerospace supply chain leaves the industry somewhat exposed. Although Chinese imports are not specifically disclosed by the manufacturers, most source components internationally, and virtually all cite trade wars and tarifs as a concern. Sub-suppliers are more likely to be more directly exposed to China, in our view, but could still afect supply chain health. Switching to an alternative supplier is a timeconsuming process, given the regulated nature of aerospace, though we believe that the industry has likely de-risked exposure to China to a degree following the previous trade war.

Retail: Retail issuers, particularly in general merchandise categories, could be afected by tarifs, as they could increase the cost of goods associated with import products. While ultimately such increases get mitigated by price pass-through to the consumer, which can have negative volume implications, more immediately this can manifest as gross margin pressures. In prior tarif discussions, select consumer categories have been carved out to limit inflationary pressure on consumers, which could again be a consideration.

Building Materials: The potential for higher tarifs has direct implications on input prices for this sector. Its reliance on various material inputs from global suppliers leaves supply chains throughout the industry vulnerable. While it has taken steps to mitigate exposures to such shocks, existing exposure to inputs such as steel, lumber and mineral materials could weigh on margins if tarifs were to rise and companies are unable to manage these costs efectively. More broadly, higher input prices could weigh on demand if coupled with additional macro pressures on the end consumer.

Figure 18 presents a list of IG tickers that are most exposed to tarif proposals laid out by former President Trump. Our approach identifies tickers in the IG index that are most exposed to a 60% tarif on China and 10% universal tarif, as we see the risk of escalation as the greatest headwind for those firms. We use a quantitative, bottom-up approach to highlight those tickers with material import risk and overlay fundamental analyst insights to enhance the list.

## Methodology

The screen in Figure 18 was constructed using a bottom-up approach, modeled after our equity strategists (see here). The approach uses NAICS sector-level data from the U.S. Census Bureau and Bureau of Economic Analysis to screen for names that are in sectors with meaningful exposure to Chinese and global imports and exports. We then overlay suggestions from our fundamental analysts to enhance the screen we present.

## European credit: Downside to HY over IG and Autos and cyclicals

We consider the efect on European credit from the imposition of US tarifs via economic growth, monetary policy and interest rates.

We consider a scenario of 10% (additional) tarifs being added on all imports to the US, 60% tarifs on Chinese imports to the US, and retaliatory tarifs on the US. There are several mitigating factors to the initial shock, which have implications for company performance. For example, European (or, indeed, Chinese) companies, depending on their margins, could choose to absorb some or all of the higher export costs. The tarifs would thus afect company earnings and potentially capex, but also reduce the direct efect on economic growth.

What do our economists and rates strategists see as implications from such a scenario? For Europe, the assumption is a growth shock to the euro area of -0.7% after one year (relative to a baseline 2025 assumption of 1.2%). Unlike for the US, tarifs would not be inflationary, with only a 0.1% increase to the baseline inflation forecast. In short, for Europe, the tarif scenario is very much a growth shock, as opposed to a stagflation shock. In line with this, our economists assume 50bp more cuts in 2025 relative to the baseline. In turn, our rates strategists see the tarif scenario as bullish for European duration, with 10y Bunds being able to break well below 2%.

We consider macro credit and sector implications. As always with 'known unknowns', our views reflect a combination of what could happen and what is the current compensation for such an event.

Macro level: Short-term headwind for IG, medium-term headwind for HY For European IG, we see three aspects tilting short-term cautious, medium-term sanguine:

1. European IG is highly sensitive to rate volatility (and volatility in general), whether homegrown or imported (Figure 20). Faced with a growth and (for the US) inflation shock, there could be heightened rate volatility, which could cause IG spread widening in the short term.

2. European IG has shown itself to be a large beneficiary of high all-in yields in the past three years, attracting demand from retail, given valuations vs. equities (Figure 21), and pension funds. Should yields drop further, this could be a medium-term headwind to demand. That said, we believe that investors will ultimately 're-anchor' their yield expectations and demand for credit will resurface.

3. The scenario is very much in the 'negative correlation' camp: a negative shock to growth, but no inflation increase, allowing the ECB to lower rates. This should help the total return prospects of IG credit: spreads may widen (but potentially not by much, as in our base case there wold be no recession), but government bond yields could drop meaningfully.

FIGURE 20. European credit spreads are highly correlated to rates volatility
![](images/65fc352d98f7070038fad94f1ed46524a2b442ca79273cac28b032971df1eac7.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 21. High all-in yields have helped credit attract demand since 2022; recently, IG yields have dropped vs. dividend yields, causing concerns about demand for credit
![](images/99900f9929624653a255f69830652c8fd67eb6cc8204506d126d5593c83ba68b.jpg)
€IG corporate yield vs. SXXP dividend yield. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research
For European HY, we see a number of aspects tilting cautious:

1. Valuations vs. IG are not particularly attractive, at least not on a straight spread level comparison (Figure 22). There are arguments for why HY valuations are not as unattractive as they optically appear (in particular, given the much shorter average maturity now) but - at best - HY is fair, and most likely rich to IG.

2. Lower growth prospects would, in our view, hurt HY companies more than IG, serving as a decompression impulse. This is particularly the case since, even if rates were to drop, refinancing costs would most likely be higher than those for the maturing debt. This would be felt particularly strongly as there are many HY bonds up for refinancing in the coming years, with little progress on the refinancings so far (Figure 23).

3. The combination of growth downside and still-high financing costs could lead to continued high levels of idiosyncratic risk, but also pose a general headwind as fundamentals deteriorate and secondary is repriced by new issues.

4. Assuming recession concerns from the tarif shock ease in the medium term, the lower all-in yields could lead to crossover demand for mainly BB rated credit.

FIGURE 22. European HY is tight to €IG on a long-term basis
![](images/f9fb1394885a56988ffcd0214d8ce2cbcfe615e6ca266a7c0f8b1732cef98ff7.jpg)

## Implications for cyclical risk and the automotive sector

At a sector level, we see tarif risk through two angles:

1. Downside risk to growth: Cyclical risk across IG and HY credit is not well priced, in our view (Figure 24). Away from general economic uncertainty, the risk of tarifs supports our caution on cyclical risk in IG and HY.

2. Downside risk to autos: This is through two principal channels: direct exposures through European exporters to the US and through exporters to China as Chinese growth drops. We do not believe that investors are well compensated for taking auto risk (Figure 25), which makes us cautious on this sector, for IG and HY.

FIGURE 24. Cyclical premia in IG and HY remain very low vs. history (bp)
