## Global Equities

Venu Krishna, CFA BCI, US  |  Emmanuel Cau, CFA Barclays, UK

## US Equities: Trade policy implications

During his term as president, Trump imposed 10-50% tarifs on \$370bn in Chinese imports. He also imposed export restrictions on the sale of US semiconductors to select Chinese companies, framed as a response to China's unfair trade practices and intellectual property theft. As a result, the average US tarif on imports from China rose from 3.1% in January 2018 to 21.0% in December 2019 (Global Trade: Back to the bad old days? June 20, 2024). China responded titfor-tat, raising the average tarif on US imports to 21.8% from 8.0%. President Biden campaigned against these tarifs in the lead-up to the 2020 US presidential election, but has mostly kept them in place after multiple delays in the USTR review.

In US trade policies towards China, our Public Policy analyst discussed the diferences and similarities in likely trade policies in a second Biden or Trump term. We would expect Vice President Harris to follow an approach similar to President Biden in regards to trade policy. Former President Trump's election could present the risk of higher tarifs, though admittedly it can be challenging for investors to predict what policies he ultimately may impose, given the fluidity of his decision-making and the input he receives from advisers. In campaign speeches and media interviews, Trump has said he is considering imposing 60% or higher tarifs on Chinese imports to the US, a universal 10% tarifs on all imports, reciprocal tarifs on countries that impose tarifs on US exports, and 100% tarifs on autos imported from Mexico (targeting Chinese electric vehicles), among other policies.

As was the case in 2018, we would expect retaliatory action (especially China) with similar tarifs on US exports. We would also expect other measures similar to those threatened during the 2018-19 trade war, including export restrictions and sanctions on international operations of US companies, which could hurt corporate profits.

## Pricing power and supply chain flexibility help difuse the hit to profit margins

We revisit the Trump tarif screen that we had introduced in Election 2024: Impact on US Equities, June 18, 2024. Our original screen applied a top-down approach, leveraging trade and gross output data from the US. Census Bureau and Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA) for the 163 NAICS industries corresponding to S&P 500 stocks. A key assumption we had made was that US companies bear the entire brunt of the tarifs on imports and exports. Thus, for example, we had assumed that US exporters will not increase prices for foreign customers to account for higher retaliatory tarifs (ie, they do not have pricing power as exporters). Similarly, we had assumed that US importers will also absorb the higher costs of components/finished goods that they buy from their foreign suppliers. In reality, this is an overestimate because US companies could follow some of the same tactics they used to deal with the higher tarif costs during the 2018-19 US-China trade war:

• Pass higher costs onto their customers and accept some decline in market share. While this is less of a possibility for commoditized products, as consumers can switch easily (unless competitors face the same tarif headwinds), pricing can be adjusted for more diferentiated products so as to optimize profitability and will depend on the demand elasticity of the product.

• Importers force suppliers to eat the higher tarif costs by lowering the prices of their supply parts/finished goods to the company. This will depend on the company’s supplier power, availability of alternate suppliers and ability to switch suppliers easily.

• Shift supply chain and production to other low-cost countries ('China+1' strategy). Sourcing outside of China depends on the flexibility of the company’s supply chain and if they have already shifted some of their production in low-cost countries away from China.

• The last resort is simply to absorb the tarifs as a hit to profit margins while keeping product prices unchanged to protect/grow market share.

US companies have dealt with the increased costs from tarifs by adopting a combination of the above four strategies. It is dificult to assess the efect of the first three using a top-down approach, since this would be dependent on precise supply and demand elasticities. Moreover, our analysis had focused on companies that directly import or export. In reality, the cost of tarifs is likely spread across diferent parts of the supply chain, depending on who has the most pricing power. Then, there are industries facing competition from foreign companies that undercut US pricing by manufacturing goods in low-cost markets and importing them into the US. These stand to benefit from increased American protectionism.

In revisiting this theme, we have adopted a bottom-up approach to assess the tarif exposure of S&P 500 stocks by cross-checking our top-down estimates for import/export versus sales ratios with factual inputs from Barclays’ sector analysts (please see U.S. Equity Strategy: Election 2024: Trade policy implications for details). We were especially diligent about companies for which our initial estimates of the tarif exposure were high and that were able to mitigate their tarif costs by adopting a combination of the above four strategies. Our analysts’ feedback was based on a mix of guidance provided by company managements and discussions of tarif efects either on the company's earnings calls or in their financial reporting. This screen consists of 64 names.

Most of these names in the screen belong to the Discretionary, Technology, Industrials and Healthcare sectors, which are highly dependent on global supply chains.

## Second-order efects of growth slowdown could amplify direct tarif costs

Using the assumptions stated earlier, we estimate the direct efect of the new proposed tarifs on the 2025 consensus earnings estimates for S&P 500 companies. We find that they will be negatively afected by 3.2% if the new Trump tarifs are enacted and another 1.5% if those countries retaliate with similar measures. The Materials, Discretionary, Industrials, Technology and Healthcare sectors appear to be most at risk, given their strong dependency on global supply chains.

FIGURE 26. Impact of potential Trump tarifs on US imports
![](images/5216c5d29b0a9a58f8d6088e836f17c9e0423900332b3844ae6dcbea6a3f1775.jpg)
We assume companies are able to pass through half of the cost of higher tarifs to their customers and take a hit on profit margins on the rest. We have assumed that any type of fuel (eg, oil, gas, petroleum) will not be in the list of tarifed products. Source: LSEG Data & Analytics, Bloomberg, US Census Bureau, BEA, Barclays Research
FIGURE 27. Potential retaliatory tarifs by foreign countries

![](images/8494ce60906718d26a1fa67dc54857316593d2c0e71002d1cc09557cefac7b70.jpg)
We assume companies are able to pass through half of the cost of higher tarifs to their customers and take a hit on profit margins on the rest. We have assumed that any type of fuel (eg, oil, gas, petroleum) will not be in the list of tarifed products. Source: LSEG Data & Analytics, Bloomberg, US Census Bureau, BEA, Barclays Research

The higher tarif costs would be likely inflationary as increases in US import prices lead to higher prices for domestic consumers buying finished goods and producers that buy imported intermediate goods. This would add pressure to already elevated inflation in the US and hurt the final demand of goods, leading to a slowdown in US economic growth. US dollar appreciation and trade retaliation would also reduce demand for US exports. Barclays economists estimate that a 10% universal tarif, along with a 60% tarif on China, would increase PCE price levels 0.9pp over the first year. They also estimate a 1.4% negative efect on US GDP in the first year due to the higher tarifs, assuming symmetric retaliation from afected countries.

While the new proposed tarifs would have a modest direct negative efect on corporate earnings if implemented, the second-order efects from higher cost inflation and slowing economic growth would be an incremental headwind to corporate earnings and cause further pain.

Figure 28 shows the average return of the stocks within the Trump tarif screen; it consists of large-cap stocks that are most exposed to his proposals. The average return of these stocks was higher than the SPW in the first half of the year, but gave back almost all of their gains after the debate between Biden and Trump. That debate led Biden to suspend his campaign, which likely caused markets to price a higher probability of an escalation in trade tensions. The average return of the stocks has remained range-bound in the past two months, as the presidential race remains tight.

## European Equities: Bad for sentiment, but efect on stocks limited Tarifs are the main implication for Europe from US elections

Since President Biden withdrew from the race, the outcome of the election has become much more uncertain. In fact, VP Kamala Harris has gained a lead over Trump in the latest online prediction markets, after the debate on September 10, but the election remains a toss-up. Meanwhile, the chances of a Republican sweep of the Senate and House have declined, according to betting markets.

FIGURE 29. Current polls and betting averages show the race between Trump and Harris is too tight to call...
![](images/dcc3dec6079053b1c7a39cefd350d7a29edc8891944dc66138cfc1c3674886e9.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

![](images/7ce743f86f83af1bc4c7caa1a7c762a4a7f2854243396b297c112bb3ba84a57f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Both candidates have been generally vague on policies, but they have spoken in detail on a few areas that could be key for the economy and equity markets. In a broad sense, Harris wants to provide tax relief to the middle class, while raising taxes for high earners and corporations. However, these policies would need the approval of Congress and, hence, would likely require a blue sweep to be implemented. Trump's policy indicates tax cuts for corporations further from 21% to 20% (and 15% for manufacturers with American workers) and extending 2017 personal tax rate cuts. This again would require approval from Congress and, hence, may require a red sweep to be executed. On trade and tarifs, Trump’s approach is harder, proposing a minimum tarif of 10-20% and 60% on goods from China, while Harris would be expected to stay more in line with status quo. From an equity market standpoint, regulatory/tax risks from a Harris presidency and Blue sweep scenario and tarifs implementation from a Trump presidency pose risks. So, all things considered, based on the current policy proposals from the respective candidates, a Harris presidency with a split Congress is likely to be the more equity-marketfriendly scenario.

Indeed, even if Republicans control the presidency and the Senate, but not the House, tarifs may still be one of the few policies that Trump, if elected, can deliver. This would have major implications for Europe, given its high exposure to global trade, although the euro area trade balance with the US in GDP terms has come down in the past few years and stands at \~1.1%. Sectors that contribute the most to the EU trade surplus with the US, hence are likely to be the most exposed to tarifs, are Machinery/Transport Equipment, Chemicals, Automotive Products and Food, Beverages and Tobacco.

## Second-order tarifs efects might translate into a high single-digit drag on European EPS

According to our economists' analysis of the efect of a potential trade war on US, China and EU GDP, the initial loss could be largest for China, followed by the US, but would be more persistent for Europe and other open economies. For the euro area, they highlight that the direct efects on GDP could be limited at -0.1%, as the region's competitiveness would benefit somewhat from a higher tarif on China and no tarif of non-US countries on the EA. However, there could be a persistent negative efect on growth from trade policy uncertainty, especially for more open economies such as the EA and China. In aggregate, taking into account the direct efect and trade policy uncertainty, they see negative GDP hits of  -1.4% on the US, -0.7% on Europe, and -2.0% for China.

On prices, they expect inflation to rise in the short run, especially in the US, by about 1.0pp, as the negative supply shock from higher tarifs increases prices. It would then revert after the tarif increases have passed through prices and demand weakens over time. In the EA, the inflationary efects would much more limited (0.1%), while in China, the initial increase (0.4%) could then be more than ofset by the weaker growth. The stagflationary efect of US tarifs would, thus, add woes to an already fragile global economy and complicate matters for central bank policy, in our view.

Top-down, we use nominal GDP in our earnings models, as including inflation in the growth data better captures what is happening at the top line (sales) and therefore is a more accurate input for modeling EPS at the bottom line. Below, we use our earnings model estimates of sensitivity to nominal GDP and split it by real GDP and inflation; arguably, while a tarif war might be growth negative, it is likely to be inflation positive, which could ofset this somewhat. With our economist's estimates of -0.7% and 0.1% for eurozone GDP and inflation respectively, for Euro Stoxx (SXXE), we would expect a high single-digit drag on EPS growth. For 2025, IBES consensus estimates are for near double-digit EPS growth for Europe, led largely by Cyclicals. In the worst-case scenario of a full-blown tarif war with retaliations, most of the growth expectations for next year is likely to be shaved of.

## Direct efect on European companies likely smaller

Bottom-up, our analysts see a limited efect on their coverage from the possible 10% US tarifs on European imports. First, a large amount of Construction Materials and Cap Goods European companies generating revenues in the US are actually producing goods onshore, making the US tarifs less relevant. Second, following the 2018 tarifs, some European businesses, in Machinery notably, reallocated some of their supply chain to the US. Hence, a new round of US tarifs might have a lower efect than the 2018 one.

Also, Trump's higher tarif on China versus EU/RoW could make producers in China less competitive. Indeed, our Chinese economists highlight that there has already been a high degree of trade diversion via Southeast Asia and LatAm after the 2018 tarif implementation. In fact, some EU countries such as the Netherlands, Sweden and Germany could benefit from

direct investment from Chinese companies. Meanwhile, our sector analysts suggest that USdomiciled producers for European names in Metals & Mining and Chemicals could be minor beneficiaries from a less competitive China. At the same time, European firms that export to the US from a production base in China will be adversely afected. Figure 42 summarises the main efects of potential US tarifs on the European sectors:

In 2018, Defensives outperformed Cyclicals when tarif headlines hit the tape. We see Capital Goods, Autos, Beverages and Chemicals most at risk from tarif implementation, given their higher exposure to the US. However, our analysts see a mostly minor earnings/margins hit, given many firms have increased their production in the US. We launched a basket of SXXP stocks that could be afected the most from tarifs (BCEUTRAD) and it has been closely tracking Trump's likelihood of a victory so far. Similarly, Chinese exposed stocks (BCEUCHIN basket) have been weighed to some extent by the possibility of Trump returning as president and may continue to struggle if he manages to win.

China

# Escaping Japanification

China faces mounting structural headwinds as a deflating housing bubble upends the growth model, ravages household/ firm balance sheets and spurs deleveraging that may be only half done, fuelling concerns about a Japan-style balance sheet recession. A switch to new export-focused growth drivers is adding to trade frictions.

Previously published as China: Escaping Japanification, 22 July 2024.

• Economic miracle no more. China's economic development over the past four decades was remarkable, with the economy growing 250-fold from 1978-2020, lifting 800mn people out of poverty, and becoming the world's largest manufacturing hub. This transformation was driven by trade liberalisation, integration into global supply chains, favourable demographics, fast urbanisation and rapidly rising leverage. China's accelerated economic development was reminiscent of Japan's postwar economic miracle. Moreover, China was in certain quarters once expected to overtake the US as the world's largest economy by 2035. However, after decades of rapidly narrowing the gap to the US, since 2022 China has started losing ground. Surpassing the US economy now appears a distant hope; its weakening labour market, declining firm profitability, slumping housing activity, and adverse debt-deflation dynamics have raised concerns about China's longer-term growth outlook.

• Some slowdown was unavoidable. Previous achievements were accompanied by a build-up of financial imbalances and macro vulnerabilities. China's rapid credit expansion and skyrocketing home prices have long been a cause of great concern, with its credit-to-GDP gap flashing warning signals as early as 2015-18. To contain growing financial risks, China opted to prick the housing bubble, with the introduction of its Three Red Lines policy in 2020. As the bubble deflated and asset prices slumped, the risk associated with high leverage started to be widely recognised by economic agents. Households and corporates acted to shore up their balance sheets by 1) selling assets, and 2) cutting consumption and investment. While such behaviour made sense at the level of the individual or firm, its collective impact was disastrous for the broader economy, leading to shrinking aggregate demand.

• A widespread and painful reversal in wealth-creation dynamics. Unlike the huge positive wealth efect seen in the US post COVID, Chinese households have sufered a massive loss of wealth from the housing slump, amounting to an estimated USD18trn. To put that in perspective, it is as if each 3-person household in China has since 2021 lost \~USD60k (almost 5 times China's per capita GDP). While it is true that wealth efects are unequally distributed, we would argue that because home ownership levels were so high and the drop in home

prices since 2021 has been so significant and widespread, the negative wealth efects are likely more evenly spread out in China's case. With such a large headwind facing China's economy, it is very dificult to see a quick and sustained rebound in consumption, and we envisage a disappointing growth recovery.

• This phenomenon has occurred in other places. China is not the first country to face slowing growth and a painful deleveraging process: Japan, Thailand, Ireland, Spain, and the US, to name a few, have confronted similar crises as households and corporates shied away from borrowing and began to accelerate loan repayments, even in the face of monetary and credit policy easing. International experience suggests it could easily take a decade or longer to repair national balance sheets after such a crisis. For example, it took Japan more than two decades to bring private-sector leverage down, while it took the US and Spain almost a decade to repair their balance sheets. Based on these overseas experiences, we think China's deleveraging journey has only just started, and it is unlikely to be completed before 2030, which implies the structural headwinds to consumption and investment will persist.

• China faces structural headwinds. The economic circumstances facing China have led some to question whether the nation is heading towards a similar situation as faced Japan after its asset bubble burst in the late 1980s (Figure 1). We think that while Japan’s experience ofers some insights and lessons, China is confronting a set of challenges that is in many ways unique.

° <sup>First,</sup> <sup>China's</sup> <sup>demographics</sup> <sup>are</sup> <sup>less</sup> <sup>favourable,</sup> <sup>with</sup> <sup>its</sup> <sup>population</sup> <sup>decline</sup> <sup>coinciding</sup> <sup>with</sup> the bursting of its asset bubble, while Japan's population only started to decline almost two decades after its bubble burst.

° <sup>Second,</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>direct</sup> <sup>hit</sup> <sup>from</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>housing</sup> <sup>slump</sup> <sup>could</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>larger</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>China</sup> <sup>than</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>Japan</sup> <sup>given</sup> residential investment's share of China's GDP is double the ratio in Japan in the 1990s. Moreover, bubble-related indicators (ie, vacancy ratios, price-to-income ratios, rental yields, etc) look more worrisome in China than Japan in the 1990s.

° <sup>Third,</sup> <sup>China's</sup> <sup>tensions</sup> <sup>with</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>US</sup> <sup>have</sup> <sup>extended</sup> <sup>from</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>fields</sup> <sup>of</sup> <sup>economics</sup> <sup>and</sup> <sup>trade</sup> (Japan's case decades ago) to the arenas of national security and ideology. With ongoing great-power competition, we think the deterioration of China-US relations will likely be a long-term trend.

• Policy makers are pivoting to new growth drivers, but sustainability remains in question. Recognising these challenges, the authorities have focused on new areas to generate high-quality growth (re-emphasised in the Third Plenum), pivoting resources (capital and human talent) towards manufacturing – primarily high-tech sectors – and away from real estate. The new growth drivers of the Chinese economy are very diferent to the old, with industrial digitisation and green tech taking the baton. We estimate these two sectors collectively became the largest driver of China's GDP growth in 2023, accounting for 60% of the expansion of GDP that year. This means without the contribution from these two sectors, China would have seen its GDP rise by just 2% in 2023, instead of the 5.2% actually recorded. A side efect of China's dominant role in high-tech manufacturing is overcapacity and trade tensions. This means China's investment in high-tech manufacturing cannot continue indefinitely, given weak domestic demand and the less favourable external environment.

• Positively, China has time to analyse and learn from Japan’s experience. While there is no quick fix to structural issues, we think China can avoid Japan's policy missteps, particularly in terms of waiting too long to write of non-performing loans and allowing zombie firms to roll over or even add to their debts.

Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Applying those lessons, we think the government could consider the following policy options:

° <sup>Zombie</sup> <sup>companies</sup> <sup>should</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>weeded</sup> <sup>out,</sup> <sup>and</sup> <sup>their</sup> <sup>factors</sup> <sup>of</sup> <sup>production</sup> <sup>reallocated</sup> <sup>to</sup> more promising companies.

° <sup>On</sup> <sup>macro</sup> <sup>policy,</sup> <sup>to</sup> <sup>support</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>economy,</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>central</sup> <sup>government</sup> <sup>could</sup> <sup>use</sup> <sup>fiscal</sup> <sup>policy</sup> more actively, especially given the reduced willingness to consume and invest in the private sector.

° <sup>More</sup> <sup>monetary</sup> <sup>stimulus</sup> <sup>could</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>deployed,</sup> <sup>including</sup> <sup>keeping</sup> <sup>real</sup> <sup>interest</sup> <sup>rates</sup> <sup>very</sup> <sup>low,</sup> as well as unorthodox policy tools such as QE, given the increasingly large fiscal burden from ageing-related issues. We think the PBoC's Q3 moves to prevent rates from falling (targeting financial and FX stability for now) won't be efective for long, as fundamentals continue to weaken.

° <sup>Lastly,</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>order</sup> <sup>for</sup> <sup>growth</sup> <sup>to</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>sustainable,</sup> <sup>it</sup> <sup>will</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>necessary</sup> <sup>to</sup> <sup>raise</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>of</sup> <sup>return</sup> on capital through innovation and productivity growth.

FIGURE 1. China has become more like Japan in several ways: low inflation rate, low population growth and low policy rate
![](images/92a81864d89947ec4e65017ae03fb84498bf65a6593627a3e39611afdecb62eb.jpg)
To measure the phenomena of slow growth, low inflation, and low policy rate, a Japanese economist, Takatoshi Ito, introduced a Japanification Index, which measured the sum of the inflation rate, nominal policy rate, and the GDP gap. To apply to China's economy, we adjusted this index, by replacing the GDP gap with working-age population growth, as the estimation methods of GDP gaps difer across nations and working-age population is by far the most fundamental determinant for long-term growth.

## Financial imbalances: time to pay back

China's rapid credit expansion had been a cause of concern for some time, attracting attention from researchers at the IMF<sup>21</sup> , PBoC<sup>22</sup> , BIS and others<sup>23</sup> (Figure 2 and Figure 3). Specifically, the BIS looked at China's credit-to-GDP gap, defined as the diference between the debt/GDP ratio and its long-run trend, and used it to quantify the notion of excessive credit in a simple way, serving as an early warning indicator for potential financial distress. According to the BIS, a level above 10pp signals that a crisis is likely to occur within the next three years. Interestingly, China's credit-to-GDP gap rose above 10pp during 2015-18. China's property bubble burst in 2021, three years after the excessive credit indicator began flashing warning signals.

FIGURE 2. Chinese economy has built up substantial debt over the past two decades...
![](images/3126fabd8b5d3db9d33c9e1ac92f3cc68f88052e55adbb6975a1e20f9152628a.jpg)
Credit data refer to private non-financial sector, including non-financial corporations and households.
Source: BIS, Barclays Research

FIGURE 3. ...with debt services ratios (DSR) for non-financial sector hitting 22% in 2023 from \~13% before GFC
![](images/c78533bc88499414b63e8de3fa7a08c175482e3af210e9cefb0deec3eee6ca41.jpg)
Note: BIS debt service ratios which reflect the share of income used to service debt for the total private non-financial sector Source: BIS, Barclays Research

## Households: a decade to repair damaged balance sheets

China's traditionally under-leveraged household sector has seen rapid credit expansion amid several housing booms, rivalling that of Japan and the US before their bubbles started to burst. The bursting of the property bubble has exposed the vulnerability of Chinese household balance sheets, with their assets by value (mostly in property) shrinking by an average \~30% from the peak while their liabilities remained broadly unchanged.

Household balance sheets in China are overstretched now. Even with interest rates being cut to record lows, there are still not enough households willing to borrow to consume or to buy properties. Households are experiencing a painful period of balance-sheet repair, which could easily take a decade (or longer) to complete, drawing from the Japanese and US experience. We examine the state of Chinese household balance sheets from liability, asset and income sides.

• Liability side: Chinese household debt has more than doubled in the past decade, rising to a high of \~143% of disposable income in 2021 before stabilising (Figure 4). Credit demand among households was exceptionally weak in the past year. Similar observations were found in Japan and the US going into their housing crises. Moreover, accompanied by slowing income growth, China's average debt-services burden rose to 22% of household disposable income in H1 24, exceeding similar measures in all major economies. This suggests limited room to add leverage and some painful adjustment ahead, with adverse long-run efects on consumption and housing demand.

• Asset side: The property downturn has resulted in massive wealth destruction. Housing assets made up \~70% of Chinese household wealth in 2021, versus \~65% in Japan (2019) and \~25% in the US (Q4 23; Figure 5). The existing home-price data reported by Centaline Property show even the more resilient tier 1 cities saw a \~30% decline on average from their peaks in mid-2021. Applying that conservative loss rate across China's \~USD60trn residential property market as of 2021 – the high-water mark – generates wealth destruction of an estimated USD18trn, which is equivalent to an entire year of Chinese GDP, or 8 years of 2023 total household savings for China's 1.41bn population. Another yardstick: the asset value so far destroyed by the property slump is almost twice the size of the total market cap of China's equity market (\~USD9trn).

• Income side: We think the deteriorating labour market and reductions in salaries24 add another layer of pressure to vulnerable household balance sheets. The Ministry of Finance has reported that revenues from individual income taxes contracted in the 17 months up to July, and were down 5.5% in the first seven months of this year. Similarly, data from private sources suggest a marked deceleration in wage growth. Average wage growth in 38 major cities slowed sharply to 0.5% y/y in Q2, from 2.2% y/y in Q1, according to Zhilian Zhaoping (a Chinese recruitment platform). Unstable income, alongside pressure to repay existing loans, and continued anxiety about declining home prices are the top reasons holding back home purchases, according to the China Index Academy. We are seeing continued negative newsflow of pay cuts and layofs ranging from traditional sectors (eg, property) to new sectors (eg, EVs and solar panels) to financials this year<sup>25</sup> .

Amid this gloomy outlook, households have been trying to pay down their debts and are less willing (or able) to consume and invest (Figure 6), with a greater preference for saving. The value of outstanding mortgages has been on a declining trend over the past four quarters as households work to pay of home loans ahead of schedule. Credit data show the largest-ever contraction in loans to households this year.

There is an argument that suggests households, given the right incentives, could tap their excess savings to boost consumption. However, we note household savings are mostly in the form of time deposits, whose share of total deposits hit a record of 96% in 2023 (Figure 7). Anecdotal evidence suggests households prefer to hold time deposits with maturities as long as five years to lock-in relatively "high" interest rates as an environment of lower-for-longer interest rates seems widely expected<sup>26</sup> .

Corporations: deleveraging started in 2017, but may be only halfway done China's corporate sector saw a rapid build-up of leverage from 2008 to 2016, while it started to deleverage from 2017 onwards, four years before the bubble burst. Oficial data show corporate debt as a percentage of GDP declined to below 140% of GDP in 2017-23 from the peak of 148% in 2016 (Figure 8).

We think corporate deleveraging was attributable to: 1) a government-led deceleration campaign in the real economy (starting in 2017); 2) rising geopolitical tensions beginning with the trade war (2018-now); and 3) a less predictable policy space and regulatory crackdowns (intensified in 2021) on various sectors (internet, education, healthcare, etc), which weighed on investment sentiment.

We think Chinese corporates' balance-sheet repair journey may be only about halfway done, if we take Japan as an example (see section below). Private firms have become more cautious in terms of: 1) taking more loans and credit; and 2) increasing capex, despite regulators' steppedup eforts to support credit growth (Figure 10 and Figure 11). Growth in credit to corporations has fallen since April 2023, while private-investment growth almost stalled in the past year. Lower interest rates could ease some of the corporate debt-service burden, but we think the bigger challenge for deleveraging is declining corporate profits. We highlight that government revenue from corporate income taxes contracted 5% y/y YTD in July, the third straight month of contraction this year.

• In the manufacturing sector (\~30% of GDP), Chinese corporates have seen two years (2022-23) of outright contraction in profits amid low return on capital, overcapacity issues, and sluggish domestic demand. The number of loss-making manufacturing firms surged to \~144k (accounting for \~30% of total firms) in July 2024, more than double than in 2021 (Figure 9). Within manufacturing, auto makers (auto sales revenue accounts for \~4% of GDP) saw total profits slump 35% in 2023 followed by an 11% y/y decline in H1 24 amid fierce price wars, according to the CAAM. Overall, we think industrial profits are under mounting pressure this year, with the gap between PMI output and input prices nearing a two-year wide in Q2. These developments indicate that firms may be unable to pass on higher costs to customers due to subdued domestic demand, which will result in a decline in profits.

• In the real-estate sector (\~17% of GDP), many developers' net profit fell as much as 40-50% y/y in the past year<sup>27</sup> , with slumping property sales and declining margins<sup>28</sup> . Total sales value slumped by \~40% from the peak in 2021 to CNY11trn in 2023. Developers are undergoing painful deleveraging and debt restructuring, with over 70% of Chinese developers with USD bonds having defaulted as of end-Q2. We see no signs of easing in terms of profit and revenue pressures for developers this year. Property sales revenue for the top 100 developers was down \~40% y/y in the first eight month of 2024<sup>29</sup> . Moreover, we think the scale of the government-led inventory destocking plan is too small in the context of perhaps CNY28trn worth of unsold properties (estimated using the extremely elevated housing inventory-tosales ratio); ie, the CNY500bn funding accounts for less than 2% of unsold inventory.

## Government: a painful journey to ensure fiscal debt sustainability

In the past decade, China's government debt has grown rapidly, arising from the extraordinary expansion policy to combat past downcycles (2008-09, and 2015-16), where the central government promoted a forceful push to increase infrastructure spending, in which the LGFVs played a big role. Taking into account implicit LGFV debts, the IMF estimated that the augmented fiscal debt surged to 116% of GDP in 2023, far exceeding the average public debt ratio of 50-60% in EMs.

The Chinese government has already embarked on a strong austerity drive – a pro-cyclical fiscal tightening at a time when bold fiscal stimulus is necessary to lift sentiment and growth. This policy push became more visible after China's top leaders explicitly called for "getting used to belt-tightening" at the December Central Economic Work Conference in 2023. The "belttightening" call was re-emphasised at the annual NPC meeting in March as well as by various local governments in H1 24.

It is also worth noting that the "belt-tightening" campaign has happened against the backdrop of 1) the collapse in land sales revenues, which accounted for \~30% of total LG revenues in 2020 before the property bubble burst (Figure 12), and 2) medium-term fiscal sustainability concerns arising from the widening pension gap. Due to the increasing fiscal burden from an ageing population, the state-run Chinese Academy of Sciences estimates that the state pension system will run out of money by 2035<sup>30</sup> as the workforce shrinks and size of the old population surges.

To make the fiscal debt position more sustainable, local governments are pushing forward plans to: 1) lower interest rates and extend maturities on debts; and 2) cutting fiscal expenditure and raising fiscal revenue from higher charges for public services.

• Lowering interest rates and extending maturities. China oficially began its long-awaited LG/LGFV debt-swap programme in 2023, issuing low interest LG refinancing bonds (as low as 3-4%) to swap with high interest rate LGFV debts (as high as 7-10%). Moreover, thanks to accommodative monetary policy, the 10y CGB yield declined from \~3.3% in Q1 21 to a record low of 2.1% in Q3 24 (Figure 13). We think the PBoC's Q3 moves to prevent CGB yields from falling further (aiming at financial and FX stability) will not fully succeed, as the economy's fundamentals are still weakening. When the average interest rate paid on government debt is lower than nominal GDP growth, we think China's fiscal stability can be maintained even with a primary deficit as long as that deficit is limited. In addition to lowering interest costs, in Q2 24 the Chinese authorities also: 1) extended the maturities of some local government debts up to 30 years; and 2) started to sell ultra long special treasury bonds with maturities of up to 50 years.

• Cutting fiscal expenditure and raising fiscal revenue. As part of the belt tightening theme, governments have been cutting the salaries of public servants, which accounted for around on third of total fiscal expenditure in the past year<sup>31</sup> . Public servants, whose positions used to be viewed as "jobs for life", have seen significant pay reductions, payment delays, and job cuts over the past year. These economic downtrends have broadened from the rich coastal provinces to the poorer inland regions, and the more heavily indebted provinces, as well as to state-linked institutions such as schools and customs ofices. For example, some local oficials in the northern provinces have experienced 35% cuts in their overall compensation, while Guangdong province has cut the pay checks of its public servants by 25% in the past year<sup>32</sup> .

Moreover, debt-ridden local governments started to reduce subsidies on public services, such as rail fares and utilities. For instance, multiple cities announced plans in Q2 to increase the per cubic metre charge for water supply by 20-30% for households<sup>33</sup> . While such measures could ease fiscal burdens (as well as push up inflation as utility tarifs make up \~5% of the CPI basket), we think they will also squeeze consumer spending on discretionary goods and services consumption. We see signs of more cost-conscious consumers seeking better value for money and downgrading their consumption choices (see China Outlook: Consumption downgraded, deflation broadened, 23 February 2024).

## More structural challenges

While boom-bust business cycles have occurred in other economies, China faces more challenges than many other countries. These include significant structural issues in the property sector (from boom to extended bust), more challenging demographics (from dividend to drag), and more severe confrontation with the West (from the economics field to national security and ideology arenas).

## Property: from boom to bust

When it comes to China, the sector to focus on is property. We think a sustained contraction in the property sectors seems likely to continue over the next several years (Figure 14 - Figure 17). Property activity previously contracted in 2009, 2012 and then in 2014-15, yet in all three periods there followed a "priming of the pump" that sparked renewed expansion of the sector, when Chinese credit growth exploded. Nevertheless, the current housing downturn seems likely to be more lasting. First and foremost, Chinese households are already overleveraged, unlike very little leverage in the past downcycles. Second, residential real estate floor space per capita in China was by 2023 already greater than that in many advanced economies (ie, Japan, Spain, Germany). Third, the housing inventory problem is more serious this time. Fourth, urbanisation may have reached a peak, with most rural residents who are able to move to urban areas having already done so.

The direct trigger for collapsing China's housing bubble was the introduction of the 'three red lines' for the finances of property developers, an action very similar to the ceiling on real-estate lending introduced in 1990 in Japan<sup>34</sup> . Starting from 2021, China's housing market entered its worse-ever downcylce. Compared with 2021, property investment and sales were down by 25% and 38%, respectively, in 2023, while new starts recorded a deeper fall of 52% (Figure 16 and Figure 17). The NBS even stopped releasing the developers' land purchase volume data series, with the latest available figures showing it contracted 53% in 2022 alone.

Amid its longest downturn on record, the share of GDP contributed by China's real-estate sector has been shrinking in the past three years. If we include upstream and downstream contributions to GDP, we estimate the real-estate sector's share in GDP declined to \~17% in 2023, from \~26% in 2020 (before the housing downturn). The ratio of residential real-estate investment to GDP has declined from 10% in 2020 to 7% in 2023, which is still high compared with 5% Japan in 1990 (Figure 15). Given the Chinese property sector's larger share of GDP, it is inevitable that the hit to the real economy could be bigger in China than in Japan. Moreover, we think the property bubble in China's housing sector appears to be larger than in Japan.

1. Vacancy ratio: the urban residential property vacancy rate is higher than 20% in China, according to the SWUFE's estimate. This would be more than double the 9% vacancy rate in Japan in 1990. In China's case, we think this ratio is really an indication of the level of speculation in the sector. If a high proportion of properties are vacant, then many investors were likely buying them for potential capital gains, which contributed to expanding the bubble.

2. Home-ownership: China’s home-ownership is already the highest in the world: 96% according to the PBoC's 2019 China Household Wealth Survey. That compares with 60% in Japan in 1990. A high proportion of home-ownership means limited room for future housing demand.

3. Price-to-income ratio: This is essentially an afordability indicator. If the ratio is too high, housing becomes beyond the reach of the majority of the population. China's home prices are around 20 times household disposable income, based on average annual disposable income of \~CNY50k and an average apartment cost of \~CNY1mn. China's home price-toincome ratio is also significantly higher than the 11 times in Japan in 1990.

4. Rental yield: This is efectively an investment return index. If the rental yield is too low and lower than mortgage rates, then investors are paying too much for property, implying that there is a bubble forming. In China, the rental yield is 1-2% compared with \~4% mortgage rates. For comparison, the rental yield in Japan was \~3% in 1990 and is \~4% now.

## Demographics: from dividend to drag

Favourable demographic trends had been major driving forces for China's strong growth in the past four decades, with China being the world's largest manufacturing centre, the largest consumer market for autos and electronic products, and the largest housing market. As the population declines, aggregate consumption growth is likely to be held back even though China's per capita consumption remains well below mature Western economies (see China Demographics: From dividend to drag, 13 September 2023). However, China's demographic landscape appeared to be more challenging than Japan in 1990s. The decline in China's population is coinciding with the bursting of the real-estate bubble, and its population is ageing even faster than Japan's was. We think the rapidly declining and ageing China's population will add further challenges to its economic growth trajectory.

FIGURE 18. China's population started to shrink in 2022, coinciding with the housing crisis
![](images/5e0cbe23b97a370a52a93a1574ead8e53d574063546db91b445ef06ea691169a.jpg)
Source: Wind, Barclays Research

• China's population decline coincided with the bubble burst. China's population started to shrink in 2022, around the same time that China's housing bubble burst. For comparison, Japan's population only started to decline in 2011, almost two decades after its bubble burst in 1991, while its working age population peaked in 2002 before declining. Our analysis of China's demographic data suggests the total population may shrink by \~130mn by 2040, reaching 1.27bn in 2040 (Figure 18 and Figure 19). That reduction is even greater than the total population of Japan and would see China's population returning to a level last seen in 2000.

• China's population is ageing at a faster pace than Japan. We expect China’s ageing population share to rise rapidly in the next decade, accelerating from 2027 onwards (Figure 20). We estimate that by 2030, more than 20% of the population will be over 65, marking China's entry into the ranks of super-ageing countries. In other words, it will have taken just nine years for China to transition from “deep ageing” to “super ageing”. For comparison, it took Japan 11 years (1994 - 2005) to make the same transition, where the faster-ageing population, along with declining population, resulted in a rapid decline in the working age population, which was already on its shrinking path in 2014.

## Geopolitics: moving from economics to national security and ideology

The Sino-US trade friction, which has intensified since Trump's administration, is reminiscent of the Japan-US friction seen 30 years ago. The underlying factor common to the bilateral trade friction is trade imbalances. China has replaced Japan as the largest trade deficit source for the US since its WTO accession in 2001 (Figure 21 and Figure 22). In 2018, before the trade war, the US trade deficit with China accounted for \~50% of its total. For comparison, the US deficit with Japan made up as much as \~65% (the peak) of overall US trade in 1991. Such large and persistent US trade imbalances with China reflected: 1) US domestic excess consumption; and 2) China's excess savings (with savings exceeding investment), similar to Japan's situation at the time of its friction with the US.

Along with the elevated trade imbalance, China's economy more broadly was catching up with the US, with its GDP reaching 60% of US GDP in 2014, and rising further to \~75% of US GDP in 2021. For more than a century, no US competitor has reached 75% of US GDP, including Japan. When one adjusts for the relative price of goods (PPP-based), China's economy was already 27% larger than the US economy in 2023 (Figure 23). Moreover, the competition between China and the US is a multidimensional contest, involving technology, global influence, political elements and the military.

Against this backdrop, we think China is facing a more challenging external environment now than Japan did decades ago, given the view of China as a potential threat to the US has extended from the field of economics (as was Japan's case decades ago) to the arenas of national security and ideology.

In recent years, we have seen a new, largely bipartisan, hawkish policy consensus emerge in the US towards China. This is characterised by national-security protectionism and industrial policy. Moreover, we think Sino-US frictions could escalate further under a potential Trump 2.0 presidency. We estimate Trump's proposed 60% tarifs on all Chinese goods would result in a \~5pp decline in China's total export growth, and reduce China's GDP growth by \~1pp (Figure 24, see Economics: Drag on China growth; maybe a win for others, 18 April 2024).

## Transitioning from housing to high-tech manufacturing

Facing such structural challenges, the authorities have focused on achieving high-quality growth (re-emphasised in the Third Plenum) by shifting resources (capital and human talent) towards manufacturing – primarily in high-tech sectors – and away from real estate.

A number of China experts, in academia and policy circles, have said repeatedly that the government wants to pursue the "Germany model", characterised by strong manufacturing capacity and export orientation based on first-class products and services<sup>35</sup> . While the government has tried to play down "Made in China 2025" (a state-led industry policy, Figure 25) since the trade war with the US, we note China still rolled out various industrial measures in the form of mobilising SOEs, using government subsidies, introducing favourable lending policies, and acquiring intellectual property to catch up with and then surpass western technological prowess in high-tech industries. It is worth highlighting that new bank loans to the real-estate sector declined in 2023 and Q1 24, in contrast to the steady increase in loans to industry, led by high-tech sectors (Figure 26 and Figure 27).

Pro-high-tech industrial policies bear fruit. By global market share, China leads the world in various high-tech sectors. For example, from 2005 to 2022, China's global market share of solar panel production rose from 11% to 88%, its global market share of lithium batteries rose from almost zero to 63% in 2022, and its global share of wind power equipment increased from 2% to 60% (Figure 29). China has marked similar growth in other high-tech/high-value-added sectors, such as industrial robots, civilian drones, and high-speed trains. On an aggregate level, China's global share in manufacturing rose significantly from below 10% two decades ago to over 30% in 2022, which is larger than the combined shares of the US, Japan and Germany (\~26%). This is a big achievement (Figure 28).

## ‘Green tech’ and industrial digitalisation taking the baton

The new growth drivers of the Chinese economy look quite diferent now, with industrial digitalisation and green tech taking the baton. These two are linked; collectively we estimate they became the largest driver of China's GDP growth in 2023, at 60%. This means without the contribution from these two sectors, China would have seen its GDP rise by just 2%, instead of the 5.2% recorded (Figure 30).

• Green tech (9% of GDP): China's green tech sector, covering solar power, wind power, EVs, nuclear power, electricity storage and hydrogen, contributed a record CNY11trn to China's economy in 2023 (\~30% y/y growth in 2023), according to the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air (CREA). This translated to 9% of GDP in 2023, up from 7% in 2022, and contributed 2.2pp to headline GDP growth of 5.2% in 2023, by our estimate. The rising importance of green tech sectors gives China a significant economic stake in the global transition to clean energy technologies.

Within the green tech sector, the three largest sectors by value are solar power, EVs, and batteries – the so-called 'New Three' – which collectively accounted for \~6% of GDP (Figure 31). In particular, China has dominated the manufacturing and installation of solar panels for years. Solar power was the largest contributor to green tech, with its combined new investment and value of production growing from CNY1.5trn in 2022 to CNY2.5trn in 2023 (\~2% of GDP), an increase of \~65% y/y, and the combined investment and value of production of EVs rose to CNY3.5trn in 2023, accounting for \~3% of GDP. Production of EVs posted 36% y/y growth, to 9.6mn units, in 2023 (Figure 32). Moreover, China is a major producer of most of the minerals required for green-tech sectors (Figure 33).

• Industrial digitalisation (8% of GDP): Industrial digitalisation involves the integration of technologies such as IoT and big data into traditional manufacturing processes, which is an important pillar of high-quality development emphasised in the Third Plenum. These include information and communication technology (ICT) and the added value that ICT brings to other industries. The value added of China’s industrial digitalisation rose 12% y/y, to CNY10trn, in 2023, and accounted for 8% of GDP, according to the NDRC. We estimate industrial digitalisation contributed 1pp to headline GDP growth of 5.2% in 2023.

We recently travelled across China with our Equity European Capital Goods colleagues, and left impressed by China's industrial digitalisation and automation eforts. China has moved up the supply value chain to much more sophisticated oferings. On factory automation, domestic substitution is a trend. Chinese suppliers have been gaining market share in automation products – even in robotics, they also have a strong market share despite being launched only a few years ago. Chinese suppliers believe their products can succeed in emerging markets, but for developed markets, further initiatives, such as R&D centers, production bases, and engineering support from the government, are needed (see European Capital Goods: China Field Trip Takeaways, 3 June 2024).

## Downside risks – overcapacity and trade tensions, and hence hard to sustain

A side efect of China's dominant role in manufacturing is that it led to excess capacity, which heightened trade frictions with the West and emerging markets. This means China's investment in high-tech manufacturing cannot continue indefinitely, given weak domestic demand and the less favourable external environment. From a GDP-expenditure perspective, we think it will be very challenging for China to rely on exports to sustainably compensate for a lack of domestic demand. In the past three decades, the net exports contribution never exceeded 1.1pp for two consecutive years, while the drag from the real-estate sector (one of the main pain points of weakened domestic demand) alone amounted to \~2pp in 2023, and is likely to stay at \~1.5pp in coming years.

• Overcapacity overhang. China's long history of overcapacity in manufacturing industries has intensified in recent years to include high-tech sectors. For example, China's EV production capacity in 2025 is expected to reach more than 36mn vehicles, a 20mn surplus over expected sales in that year, according to the publicly announced production capacity by Chinese auto OEMs. On solar power, at the end of 2023, China's annual production capacity for finished solar modules was more than double the global module installations, according to China Photovoltaic Industry Association data.

• Increased trade friction. The negative consequences of international trade, such as disappearing manufacturing jobs in advanced economies (which went mainly to China) and the associated pressure on middle-income workers, have generated increased criticism. Oficials in the West have grown increasingly mistrustful of China in particular. The world appears to be turning toward more government intervention in trade via restrictions and industrial policies, as evidenced by recent measures targeting China implemented by the US and Europe. In our view, risks are skewed toward further fragmentation of global trade and investment, especially in the case of a new Trump administration, which has said it has plans for much higher tarifs (see Global Trade: Back to the bad old days?, 20 June 2024).

## China’s diversified export market could mitigate downside risks

Although the threat of a heightened trade war remains a headwind, we think China's diversified and broadening export markets, innovation and cutting-edge technology and products, and the new wave of Chinese companies going global will continue to lend support to Chinese (high-

tech) exports and its manufacturing sector, especially in volume terms. In particular, an examination of exports and overseas investment data reveal some interesting patterns (see China Exports: An enduring bright spot, 7 June 2024):

1. Chinese exporters shipped more products to emerging markets, led by the BRI economies and neighbouring countries, including Vietnam, Malaysia, Russia, Saudi Arabia and the UAE. On the other hand, China’s share of exports to the G3 is on a declining trend (Figure 34). We see evidence of a high degree of China trade diversion via Southeast Asia countries and Mexico (Figure 35).

2. Chinese manufacturers have increased the export competitiveness of their high-tech products, supported by a decade of industrial upgrading, technological innovation, and customer-centric approaches, as seen in their rising shipments of high-end/high-tech products. In particular, growth in exports of machinery, transport, electrical equipment, and semiconductors continues to exceed headline export growth by a wide margin.

3. While they remain cautious, many Chinese manufacturers are steadily expanding and investing in overseas markets to gain global market share amid geopolitical tensions and slowing domestic growth. Specifically, Asean countries (Vietnam, Thailand), LatAm (Mexico, Brazil), and the EU (Netherlands, Sweden, Germany) are the top destinations for China's overseas direct investment (ODI). Cross-country data show that faster ODI growth tends to be associated with faster export growth (Figure 36 and Figure 37). And China has become the largest FDI source country in Thailand, Malaysia, Cambodia, East Africa, Brazil, Hungary and Serbia.

4. A still-competitive currency, as measured by the real efective exchange rate, arguably helps to avoid undermining China’s competitiveness in the global manufacturing market (Figure 38). Due to deflation in China versus inflation in the rest of the world, China's REER depreciated by over 10% from the recent peak in March 2022. We think the CNY remains under depreciation pressure (a still competitive currency) given the elevated US-China interest rate diferential and potential tarif hikes.

FIGURE 34. China's exports to BRI countries surpassed shipments to the G3 economies
![](images/a305ce3e9552e559fd0bbde17882c20466a5769ae9d7538a0d7fa6e6d0089181.jpg)

## Lessons for China from Japanification

The economic circumstances facing China have parallels with Japan's experience after its asset bubble burst in the early 1990s. This created the term 'Japanification', which is typically defined as a combination of slow growth, low inflation, and a low policy rate, accompanied by deteriorating demographic trends. To measure this phenomena, a Japanese economist, Takatoshi Ito, introduced a Japanification Index, which measured the sum of the inflation rate, nominal policy rate, and GDP gap. To apply to China's economy, we have adjusted this index, replacing the GDP gap with working-age population growth, as the estimation methods of GDP gaps difer across nations and working-age population is by far the most fundamental determinant for long-term growth. Our amended index shows that China's economy has become more 'Japanised' than Japan's recently, albeit marginally (Figure 1). This not a surprise to us. A demographic drag<sup>36</sup> , the emergence and collapse of asset bubbles, debt overhang, zombie companies, deflationary pressures from excess capacity/high debt, and high youth

unemployment, to name a few, are some of the notable similarities between the economies of China and Japan post their bubbles.

## Japan's boom-bust cycles

• Pre-bubble burst: From mid-1985 through to 1990, the 'animal spirits' of Japanese companies (both financial and others) peaked as the economy reached the culmination of its “demographic bonus”, which was powerful enough to overcome the substantial JPY appreciation after the 1985 Plaza Accord, triggered by intensifying trade tensions with the US. At that stage, Japanese companies (fueled by expectations of strong future growth) had high borrowing demands, which meant monetary policy was a powerful tool to boost the economy.

To prevent the JPY's fast appreciation from exerting severe damage on the economy, the BoJ was forced to lower its policy rate substantially. The combination of these factors fueled a self-reinforcing feedback loop of easier credit conditions, rising asset prices and ever-higher asset-price expectations, which generated exceptionally large asset bubbles (both land and equity prices) in the late 1980s. When an economy is undergoing asset bubbles, nobody thinks they are actually in a bubble, as the 'this time is diferent' mentality tends to prevail.

• Post-bubble burst: The direct trigger that burst the land-price bubble was the introduction of credit controls on real estate lending in 1990. This could be seen as similar to China's Three Red Lines guidelines to control leverage in real-estate companies introduced in 2020. Following the collapse of the asset bubbles, Japanese companies were left saddled with “three excesses”: capacity, debt, and employment, which led to prolonged downward pressure on growth and inflation (Figure 39). From a financial standpoint, asset-price deflation (price factor) precipitated stagnation in the BoJ’s financial gap (Financial Conditions Index calculated by the BoJ to assess the current phase of the financial cycle), which suppressed growth-oriented investment via sudden stops in corporate capex plans (asset factor, Figure 40). Over time, the key driver of the economic downturn shifted to lengthy and painful deleveraging (debt factor). This process left a substantial scar on the real economy of Japan.

Together with the problem of NPLs in the financial system, downward revisions to expected GDP-growth rates prompted companies to shift to being net savers, resulting from low capex growth and leading to a sizable accumulation of cash/deposits (Figure 41). Once expectations of lower growth had been fixed in the mindsets of corporate managements, they looked to reduce fixed costs, including wages, typically by shifting to non-regular workers. They agreed with workers to prioritise job security over wage growth. As a result, low growth expectations and a decline in wage growth combined to cement low long-term inflation expectations (Figure 42).

FIGURE 39. After the bubble burst, Japanese companies were saddled with three excesses: capacity, debt, and employment...
![](images/b06f89021bc6361e3214d93538ddac306525a0155aeb9eefe1f26b7e8aa2f81b.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 40. ...amid asset price deflation, driving a sudden stop in capex and painful deleveraging
![](images/742ee4f87bf23085a4eb5b9f728eb01363b1beef75539ef1dd78d438aaaf5c82.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, BoJ, Barclays Research

FIGURE 41. Companies shifted to being net savers, resulting in sizable accumulation of cash/deposits
![](images/2db1867f59cf92595a328a8bd1fa37fbe461c706654717a8bcc58bfef94a78d8.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 42. A decline in wage growth cemented low long-term inflation expectations
![](images/4023ce22ee18cc03596fa2e21ce2491d1b46881d43bd667408c4f5bf92c918f5.jpg)

## Japan’s policy missteps

Due to the legacy of the Plaza accord and stable and low CPI inflation (despite surging asset prices), the BoJ was significantly late in identifying the bubbles (delayed recognition) and commencing policy tightening (delayed action) after lowering its policy rate substantially to address JPY appreciation, in the wake of the Plaza accord in 1985. The flip side was that BoJ was forced to tighten very rapidly, once it realised its need. The same can be said for the government. While a land price bubble was expanding from the mid-1980s, it was only at the end of the 1980s that the government finally started to respond, which in turn led to a number of tightening measures being implemented over a short period of time. Even after the collapse of the bubble, the government continued to raise some property-related taxes, which aggravated the issue.

Furthermore, during the post-bubble period, the delayed response to the nonperforming loan (NPL) problem inflicted long-lasting damage on the economy (Figure 43). Of particular note was the long lag between the bursting of the land-price bubble in the early 1990s and the onset of the financial crisis in late 1990s. This period witnessed the coexistence of zombie lending (eg,

for the real estate industry) and a credit crunch (eg, for manufacturers, Figure 44). Zombie lending steadily expanded the size of NPLs.

Zombie companies needed to be weeded out, and their factors of production should have been reallocated to more promising companies. However, Japanese banks’ close relationships with their afiliated companies via the so-called main-bank system encouraged banks to continue forbearance lending to zombie companies. As argued by Caballero, Hoshi, and Kashyap ("Zombie lending and depressed restructuring," American Economic Review, 2008), zombiedominated industries exhibited more depressed job creation and lower productivity, while the congestion efect generated by zombie companies tended to erode the profitability of healthy companies.

In retrospect, after the NPL problem emerged, the government needed to implement swift and decisive action, as Japanese banks had financial incentives to procrastinate on disposing of NPLs based on the expectations that asset prices (and their collateral value) would eventually recover. However, the government took a wait-and-see attitude of regulatory forbearance, daunted by a strong political backlash against the use of taxpayers’ money for recapitalization of a special housing loan corporation, which was the first major post-bubble NPL case in 1996. The NPL problem continued to broaden, as zombie lending continued, but it was not until 2002 that the government came up with a scheme to decisively address the issue.

FIGURE 43. Japan's policy missteps: hesitancy to write of NPLs...
![](images/f394fc02179843274b22c8bda05f15eb3f043c2a761b960b3ca7509d26f2fae3.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 44. ...and failed to address zombie lending (for real-estate industry, etc) and credit crunch (for manufacturers, etc)
![](images/b3e81d5b527f0ee4bbc5237a0b149dde1bada46042a31b1374231b8bcfaa4cfb.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

## How has Japan managed Japanification thus far?

To support the economy, fiscal measures were used actively, particularly soon after the bursting of the bubbles. During the 1990s, the government relied heavily upon public works. For example, budget allocations for public works projects surpassed JPY50trn in FY1995, almost double the amount in FY1985. However, the eficacy of public works as a stimulus measure is said to have waned, partly due to relatively large continued allocations for road maintenance/ improvement and other old-fashioned measures.

Given the increasingly large fiscal burden from issues related to Japan’s ageing population, attention gradually shifted to monetary stimulus, which added impetus to lower interest rates, aside from contributions from more fundamental factors, such as low growth and inflation expectations. Once the BoJ exhausted the room for rate cuts, it deployed unorthodox policy tools, such as quantitative easing (QE), a negative interest rate policy, and even yield-curve

control, as well as risky asset purchases (ETFs, REITs, corporate bonds, and CP, Figure 45 and Figure 46).

Finally, inflationary pressure came from the second Lewisian turning point (Figure 47 and Figure 48). Fundamentally, Japan’s labor market has been undergoing a structural and irreversible change, where most of the slack is gone and wages are becoming far more sensitive to labor supply/demand dynamics. Moreover, thanks to external forces, including a surge in commodity prices, JPY depreciation, and reopening efects from the pandemic, Japan’s inflation rose above 2%. As a result, the exit from deflation, which took root after the bubble burst, is finally in sight in Japan.

## Financial market implications for Japan

Bond market: In the aftermath of the bubble bursting and the drop in loan demand, Japanese banks’ loan-to-deposit ratio started to widen as a trend from 2000, which then was invested into JGBs (Figure 49). This continued until the BoJ’s aggressive JGB purchases under Abenomics

beginning in 2013 rapidly absorbed banks’ JGB holdings, which were replaced with excess reserves at the BoJ. With persistent flows of excess savings into JGB markets and BoJ monetary easing, JGB yields were kept under long-term downtrend despite worsening fiscal dynamics in Japan. Japanese investors also increased their foreign bond investment since early 2000s (Figure 50), which accelerated in 2016 with the BoJ’s adoption of negative interest rates (NIRP) until it was unwound amid the global bond sell-of in recent years.

FIGURE 49. Banks loan-deposit gap and JGB investment
![](images/a9ba990f304f1ebcebc35d25f5875f639934f7944c2d4b51e811c800c135c78d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 50. 10y JGB yields and foreign bond investment
![](images/4f167f7c1961a7eaea3250d6dab726bccf11bb60e627a6226a36fe7a85cf9c35.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FX markets: Relative underperformance of the Japanese economy (as shown as relative output gap) has been a structural driver of JPY weakness, especially against the outperforming USD (Figure 51). This was further exacerbated by relative yield diferentials due to the BoJ’s aggressive policy easing and delayed normalization (until recently), driving yen carry trades due to wide yield diferentials. Flow-wise, outward FDI (M&A), institutional investment (FX-unhedged bond buying) and retail investment outflows (via New NISA tax exemption scheme) all added to JPY depreciation pressure (Figure 52).

FIGURE 51. Relative output gap and USDJPY
![](images/ce68bf2a7a82e2d0801e19908a0af7102f70bf1888df0d4effa4416210a17e3d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 52. Japan’s FX supply demand and JPY NEER
![](images/c543f524a4d213042912608554632c444c9662599ad78a3a21393302dca84b05.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Xccy basis: With increasing counterparty risk against Japanese banks during Japan's financial crisis following the bursting of the bubble, a “Japan premium” surfaced in xccy basis markets during 1997-98, when Japanese institutions had to pay extra costs to fund USD in markets

(Figure 53). Such a premium disappeared after 1999, but continued investment demand for foreign assets amid low domestic yields and the current account surplus have led to a resurgence in xccy basis widening under aggressive BoJ easing since 2013 (Figure 54).

FIGURE 53. ‘Japan premium’ in 3m xccy basis
![](images/2b7b358ede71f1782d1cb11bf9243e72505a304ed850fd9db38b66f271cb547c.jpg)
Note: Calculated from FX forward rate and rate diferentials. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 54. Japan foreign bond investment and xccy basis since 2010
![](images/cbe827a0ee875435a42ff1171b24c1e454d641b0dd24eaab4d3fab0b22bf12cb.jpg)
Source: MoF, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Equity market: The Japanese equity market underwent a prolonged and large slump after the collapse of the bubbles. However, heavy reliance on monetary stimulus has finally inflated equity prices. The TOPIX rose more than threefold from the bottom in 2013 (when QQE started) to present.

Environmental, Social, and Governance (‘ESG’) Related Research: There is currently no globally accepted framework or definition (legal, regulatory or otherwise) of, nor market consensus as to what constitutes, an ‘ESG’, ‘green’, ‘sustainable’, ‘climate-friendly’ or an equivalent company, investment, strategy or consideration or what precise attributes are required to be eligible to be categorised by such terms. This means there are diferent ways to evaluate a company or an investment and so diferent values may be placed on certain ESG credentials as well as adverse ESG-related impacts of companies and ESG controversies. The evolving nature of ESG considerations, models and methodologies means it can be challenging to definitively and universally classify a company or investment under an ESG label and there may be areas where such companies and investments could improve or where adverse ESG-related impacts or ESG controversies exist. The evolving nature of sustainable finance related regulations and the development of jurisdiction-specific regulatory criteria also means that there is likely to be a degree of divergence as to the interpretation of such terms in the market. We expect industry guidance, market practice, and regulations in this field to continue to evolve. Any references to ‘sustainable’, ‘sustainability’, ‘green’, ‘social’, ‘ESG’, ‘ESG considerations’, ‘ESG factors’, ‘ESG issues’ or other similar or related terms in this document are as used in our public disclosures and not to any jurisdiction-specific regulatory definition or other interpretation of these terms unless specified otherwise.
